# Open findings register

Things measured, recorded, and **not fixed** — with no line item in any stage
of [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md).

**Why this file exists.** Each of these was written into `CLAUDE.md` or a
stage document at the moment it was found, in prose, beside the thing it was
found next to. That is the right place to explain *why* something is true and
the wrong place to keep a list: prose accumulates invisibly, and the only way
to know what is outstanding was to have been present when each one was
recorded. The register is the list; the prose stays where it is and is linked.

**Rules.** An item leaves this file by being fixed, by being scheduled into a
stage, or by being closed with a reason. **A row that is fixed moves to
Closed in the same turn** (2026-09-28: 31 of 92 rows in the open sections
were done and had never moved, so the open count read about twice the work;
`tests/test_register_hygiene.py` now refuses a done row in an open
section). **A live exposure is listed here even when a stage owns it**: a
row with a home stopped counting as a finding, and NSOT_PLAN 6.1 sat outside
the triage built to catch it (C143). It does not leave by being
forgotten.

**The open count is a claim about DEFERRED work** (the operator, 2026-09-28). A row a stage owns lives in *Scheduled out of the register*, naming the stage, and leaves the open count; the stage's plan lists it. The open sections hold only what waits: A, B, UNKNOWN, and C deferred to Stage 9. Twice the register misled us about its own size (31 done rows never moved; then 26 of 61 open rows that were stage work), and both times the fix was a rule a test enforces: `test_register_hygiene.py` refuses an open row whose bucket places it in a stage other than Stage 9.

**The stopping rule for a sweep** (the operator, 2026-09-28, applied by default). A sweep may REGISTER whatever it finds, and only A items it finds are fixed in the same pass. Everything else waits, however cheap and however near the code being edited. A items found by fixing A items had begun to feed themselves (C155, C157, C158, C160), and "we are already in the file" is how a fix grows a second fix nobody triaged. C161 is the rule's first application: found inside C160's fix, in the same function, and left. Anything recorded as *"not applied"*, *"noted, not yet
addressed"*, *"recorded as a gap"* or *"left open"* belongs here on the same
day it is written.

**A register is only current if the reader checks the head.** A stale read
of a maintained file cannot be told apart from a stale file. On 2026-09-25 this
register was read at `1b9b4d3` while the repository was four commits ahead,
and three items already closed were raised again as open. That is the failure
this file exists to prevent, arriving through the reader rather than the
document. Before acting on an item, run `git log -1 -- docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md`
and compare it with the head you are reading.

Status: **open** unless stated. Last reviewed 2026-09-28 (the triage below).

## Triage, 2026-09-28: every open row placed

**The rule (the operator's): a finding's SEVERITY is a claim about the world,
and claims about the world are measured, not assumed.** The findings that
turned out to be live exposure all read as tidying until something was
measured: C95 (masking, and it was a secret in a shared system), C141 (a code
default, and it was a published value) and C136 (cosmetic, and the import
could not be performed for 9 hours). The inverse held too (C135, C138, C142
real and harmless). So the classification depends on facts, and a row whose
severity rests on an unmeasured one is UNKNOWN until it is measured.

**The buckets, each a stated criterion:**
- **A, LIVE EXPOSURE**: true in the world now, and time makes it worse: a
  secret readable, a device unmanageable, data being lost.
- **B, BLOCKING**: something specific cannot be built or run until it is
  fixed. The row names what. If nothing is blocked, it is not B.
- **UNKNOWN**: could be A, and the fact that decides it has not been
  measured. Named with the one measurement and its cost.
- **C, EVERYTHING ELSE**: real, correctly recorded, and the tool works
  without it.

**Before sorting:** 92 rows sat in the open sections. **31 were already done**
and moved to Closed: 29 on their own row's evidence (FIXED, DONE or closed in
the status cell, never moved), and 2 measured done on the host tonight (C17's
URLs are set; E4's drift checker is enabled and ran at 05:47Z). C141 was
merged into C39, and C143 was added. **61 open.**

### Re-sort, 2026-09-28: the CURRENT buckets, and why the triage had stopped

**The operator's question: was the triage a one-time sort, or do new rows
default to C? It was a one-time sort.** The re-triage's split (functional,
lab, minor) answers WHERE work goes, and it silently replaced the severity
axis (A, B, UNKNOWN, C) for every row recorded after it: C144, C145, C147 and
C148 each got a home and no bucket. By the criterion, **C148 is B (it blocks
the onboard-to-deploy loop, the product's central one)**, and **C147 was A
when it was recorded** (latent: the next device arriving with an RW community
would have had it written into the first permanent record; fixed the same
turn).

**Now structural:** every row in a live section, "Scheduled" included, opens
its status cell with ONE bucket, `**[A|B|UNKNOWN|C …; sorted <date>]**`. B
names what it blocks and whether that is on the critical path; UNKNOWN names
the measurement that would settle it; C names its kind (F functional, L
deferred for the lab, M minor) and its home.
`tests/test_register_hygiene.py` refuses a row without one, with a control.
**A new finding gets its bucket the turn it is recorded, with the criterion
applied, never assumed.**

**The current counts (55 open):**

| Bucket | Rows | Count |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | C39, C139, C143, all deferred (L) by the operator's decision, and COUPLED (6.1 before the rotation) | **3** |
| **B, critical** | C148 (the onboard-to-deploy loop), C54 (7.2), C50 (7.3) | **3** |
| **B, later** | C10 (7.4), C7 (7.7), C60 (Stage 8), C100 (7.10's adoption), E6 (8.7) | **5** |
| **UNKNOWN** | C78, C93, each with its measurement in its row (C106 was here; corrected below) | **2** |
| **C** | 41 open, plus the six in "Scheduled" | **41** |

**Corrected the same night: C106 was never UNKNOWN, and the error was in the
instrument.** Its two "unknowns" were (2), fixed on 09-27 (`bfde4ef`,
`d7efb9d`), and (3), already read from the code and written in the row. The
triage sorted every row from a dump that CUT EACH CELL AT 420 CHARACTERS, and
C106's finding is 7,228: the answers sat past the cut. So every live row was
re-scanned in full for a bucket-changing word past the cut. C106 was the only
row hiding a FIXED, and C93's hidden measurement leaves its UNKNOWN standing
(the prober is ruled out, and the lab host's load has no history). **A bucket
assigned from a truncated read is a claim about the truncation.**

**The inverse check (anything in C that should be A or B), row by row:**
- **D8 was CLOSED in its own row and sat in "Scheduled"**, a section the
  hygiene test did not cover. Moved. The test's first extension still missed
  it (it read the third cell, and "Scheduled" keeps its status in the fourth),
  and the control against the old register is what showed that.
- **C96 became visible on every run** once E4 put the drift checker back on
  (a run whose drifted devices are drawn nowhere). Still C: it is 7.2's
  consumer, and a detection shown incompletely is not a loss. It is flagged
  in its bucket.
- The "later" B rows (C60, C100, E6) had been folded into C as "effectively
  C". The criterion makes them B; their not being on the critical path is
  said in the bucket, not by dropping them from it.
- Nothing else in C met A: no store losing data (job health 24 of 24), no
  device unmanageable (startup-check 9 of 9), and no secret readable beyond
  the three A rows.

## B. Secrets and audit surfaces

| # | Finding | Kind | Where recorded |
|---|---|---|---|
| B9 | **CONFIRMED 2026-09-26: the off-box B2 key can destroy the backups it writes, without `deleteFiles`.** Run by the operator: `rclone delete -vv --include b2probe.txt` printed `Deleted` with no refusal, the normal listing was empty, and `--b2-versions` held the hidden version. `writeFiles` allows a HIDE, and the lifecycle deletes hidden versions a day later, so a compromised NMAS erases every off-box copy within 24 h. The first test (a single-file path: exit 0, file still listed) passed while proving nothing, because rclone's single-file check needs `readFiles`, so the file was never touched. **Fix: B2 Object Lock, Governance mode, default retention 16 days: the lifecycle's hide-after (15) plus its hide-to-delete (1)**, so no hide can delete anything sooner than the lifecycle would have. (First recorded as 15, which was a day short; corrected 2026-09-26) (docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §4). The semantics are from B2's documentation as understood here, and a five-step probe plan measures them before real data depends on them. **Closes when** step 4 of that plan shows a hidden LOCKED version surviving the lifecycle, and step 5 shows a governance bypass works. | **[C, L (Object Lock enabled, by the operator's account; not re-measured here); sorted 2026-09-28]** operator's step (measure, then enable) | docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §4 |
| B10 | **Nothing checks that the bucket's lock retention covers its lifecycle window.** **Decided 2026-09-26 (operator): a read-only key**, scoped to the bucket, which is ALSO the retrieval key (docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §6): one key, on the laptop to fetch and on the NMAS to check. It may live on the NMAS because everything it can read, the NMAS already holds unencrypted. The key exists (2026-09-26), and its capabilities include `readBucketLifecycleRules`, so the lifecycle half is readable. Whether it can read the Object Lock retention is unknown until the check is built against the real response. It also passed retrieval end to end (docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md 6f), and its attempt to hide was refused with a 401. It cannot write, so it cannot undermine what it checks, and a compromised NMAS can use it only to look. A manual check in the B2 UI was rejected: it is done once and never again, and this pair is exactly what drifts when one is changed and the other forgotten. **The property to check is not "both are 15"** but **lock retention >= daysFromUploadingToHiding + daysFromHidingToDeleting**. Shorter fails, because files become deletable before the lifecycle removes them: the hole re-opening silently. Longer warns, because the lock has silently become the retention. Equal passes. **Build after** the lock is enabled and the key exists, against the real `b2_list_buckets` response: building against documented field shapes is how B6's first read reported `never` for two VMs that had images. The new key goes into `nmas-check-secret-storage`'s declared list. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §4 |
| C143 | **oxidized-web serves every device's running configuration, unauthenticated, to the whole LAN: NSOT_PLAN's 6.1, parked "on its own schedule" since 2026-09-25 and never measured again** (found by the triage's own question, which open item is true in the world now, 2026-09-28; the register searched first: no row). Measured from the laptop, an ordinary LAN host (<laptop>), by TCP connect: ports 8888 (oxidized-web), 8000 (NetBox), 3100 (Loki) and 9116 (the SNMP exporter) on the NMAS host are OPEN; only NMAS's own 5000 is filtered. `GET /nodes.json` on 8888 answered 200 with no credential asked (status and size read; no config fetched), so every device's config, with its `secret 9` hashes and the live community, is one request away for anyone on the LAN. **Two consequences:** C95's masking in NetBox protects nothing against a LAN reader while Oxidized serves the same values unmasked; and **a community rotation made now would publish the NEW value on the LAN the day it lands**, so 6.1 precedes the rotation. NetBox's API refuses without a token (403). Loki answers unauthenticated: its reads expose device logs, and whether its PUSH is open (a LAN host writing heartbeat lines that make a dead device look alive) is not measured, and is not to be measured by writing. The exporter answers anyone, so it will SNMP-query a target with the fleet's community on a stranger's behalf. **Why it had no row:** the register holds what has no stage, and 6.1 had one. A live exposure sat in a plan under "its own schedule", which is no schedule: a stage number is not a date. | **[A, deferred (L): every device's config served to the LAN, unauthenticated; sorted 2026-09-28]** **DEFERRED, lab (the operator, 2026-09-28): Stage 9's "safe here because it is a lab" half.** Real exposure, acceptable in this environment only: a lab, no hostile party on the LAN, emulated and disposable devices. It moves ahead of whatever stage is running if the tool manages anything real, if the lab becomes reachable beyond the operator's LAN, or if a device holds a credential that also works somewhere else (NSOT_PLAN Stage 9). **6.1 (C143) and the community rotation (C139) are COUPLED; whichever happens first constrains the other.** Rotating while oxidized-web still serves configs to the LAN publishes the NEW value there the day it lands. Closing 6.1 changes what reads Oxidized and NetBox (NMAS reads NetBox at the LAN address), which the rotation's consumer work must also match. Was TRIAGE A. The step, when it is taken: measure every consumer's URL first (NMAS's `oxidized_url`, the clab host's reads, Grafana's and Prometheus's sources), then bind each published port to 127.0.0.1 or add `DOCKER-USER` rules, then re-measure FROM ANOTHER HOST (6.1's own acceptance). Before the rotation. | [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md) 6.1 |
| C144 | **The daily restore test was never evidence that the ENCRYPTED copies can be read, and its green light was read that way** (the operator, 2026-09-28, closing B8). `nmas-netbox-restore-test` restores the newest LOCAL hourly backup, which is plain, and the VM holds only the public key, so it decrypts nothing: a pass proves the dump restores, and nothing about Proxmox or B2. The operator's reading ("something decrypts these") is the claim the test's NAME makes and its code does not: a restore test beside encrypted off-box copies reads as covering them. The one assertion about them is the empty-keyring decrypt (NETBOX_BACKUP 6g), proven 2026-09-28. It needs the passphrase, so no job can run it unattended. | **[C, M → Stage 9; sorted 2026-09-28, re-sorted the same day (the operator): nothing blocks on it, and "placed in 7.2" was a stage it was not in]** The HONESTY half is DONE: the restore test's own job-health row now says it covers the plain local copy only. Remaining, Stage 9: a job-health row stating when an off-box copy was last PROVEN readable by 6g and by whom, entered by the person who ran it, stale after the interval the operator sets (proposed: 90 days); the restore test's own row states that it covers the plain local copy only | [NETBOX_BACKUP.md](NETBOX_BACKUP.md) 6g, "What this does not give" |
| C145 | **Importing the backup key's OpenPGP export into an empty keyring fails, unexplained, so the `paperkey` file is UNPROVEN as a copy** (the operator's run, 2026-09-28). `gpg --export-secret-keys` gave real, iter+salt S2K-protected material for both keys (parsed: never `gnu-dummy` or divert-to-card), and `gpg --import` into an empty `GNUPGHOME` refused the cv25519 subkey (`error sending to agent: Bad secret key`), after which the decrypt said `No SmartCard daemon`, with no card configured and no stub on the laptop. Not told apart: a mistyped passphrase at the import prompt, or GnuPG's conversion of this subkey. B8 was closed by routing around it (the agent's own key files need no import). `paperkey` restores through the same import, so NETBOX_BACKUP's "restore-tested paperkey file" is a claim this measurement does not support until the paperkey passes 6g's empty-keyring decrypt. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** (M) minor, Stage 9 candidate: run 6g from the paperkey; if it fails, diagnose the import (the passphrase first, then GnuPG's version and the subkey) | [NETBOX_BACKUP.md](NETBOX_BACKUP.md) custody, 6g |
| C156 | **probe-r1a's credential override disappeared from the store with no code that removes it and no trace of what did** (found in R1's teardown, 2026-09-28; the register searched first). Measured on the host: at the teardown-prep read the override keys were `10.255.0.32` and `10.255.0.33`; the operator's clear printed `before: False`; the backup it took first (`cp -p`, so the SOURCE's mtime) shows the store last written at **17:15:28**, already without `.33`; the list deletion ran at 17:16:06 (so the operator's reading, "cleared by the list deletion", does not fit the order) and does not touch the store in code. Only `clear_device_override()` pops an override, and only retire and Abandon call it, neither run then. At 17:15:28 the app log holds only a `GET /status/10.255.0.33` poll, 16 s after the second deploy restart, and the journal nothing. **Clears are not logged** (a set is), so the store cannot say who removed what. Harmless here (the device was being torn down); the concern is that a credential can leave the store without a cause anyone can find. | **[UNKNOWN: which process wrote the store at 17:15:28, and what it dropped. The measurement: every credential-store write logged with the process, the caller and the keys it adds and removes (never values), then watch; C157's cross-process lost update is the leading candidate; sorted 2026-09-28]** | [credentials.py](../modules/credentials.py) `_load`, `_save` |

| C289 | **The nightly backup of the lab VM is when the fleet stops answering** (found 2026-10-01 measuring C93 for the operator). Every not-answering transition the reachability reader logged, per night, against the vzdump window 08:30 to 08:50 UTC (02:30 Denver): 2026-09-29, 20 of 20 inside it; 09-30, 14 of 15; 10-01, 19 of 27 (the rest are s3's own, and the operator's changes ran in the same window). At 08:38 and 08:39 tonight every device reached through s3 went not-answering together, for 7 to 30 s. vzdump reads all 250 GiB of VM 100's disk at 385 to 405 MiB/s for 10.5 to 11 minutes in snapshot mode, and in Proxmox that backup job runs inside the VM's own QEMU process. So the "freeze with no reboot" on s3 is at least partly the host imaging the VM s3 runs in, and every night's reachability and scrape figures carry it. The mechanism (the copy-before-write I/O, the QEMU main loop, or the host's CPU) is not measured. **The operator agreed (2026-10-01) to cap the nightly job's read rate.** Read on Proxmox the same day: the job is `nmas-nightly` (VMs 100 and 102, 02:30 local, snapshot mode, zstd, keep-daily 3, no `bwlimit` set) and `/etc/vzdump.conf` sets none. vzdump's `bwlimit` is in KiB/s. Apply: `pvesh set /cluster/backup/nmas-nightly --bwlimit 102400` (100 MiB/s); check: `pvesh get /cluster/backup/nmas-nightly --output-format json` shows `"bwlimit":102400`; undo: `pvesh set /cluster/backup/nmas-nightly --delete bwlimit`. Expected: VM 100's 250 GiB read takes about 43 minutes instead of 11, so the window is longer and gentler; the comparison is the not-answering transitions inside the backup window (and per hour of it) against the night before (19 of the day's 27 inside 08:30 to 08:50 UTC on 2026-10-01). **2026-10-02: it crashed a device.** r3 aborted at 08:34:21 UTC ("Critical software exception", `UNIX-EXT-SIGNAL: Aborted(6), Process = IOSD ipc task`; processor memory 276 MB of 2 GB; the operator's reading) during that night's backup. Read on Proxmox the same day, the task log of the 02:30:08 local (08:30:08 UTC) run shows the BACKUP stalling, not only reading: VM 100 went from 15% to 16% between 1m44 and 4m12 at 19.4 MiB/s read and 9.8 MiB/s written, so the stall ended at 08:34:20 UTC, one second before r3 aborted; two more VM 100 stalls ran 63 s and 72 s, and VM 102 stalled 2.8 minutes (2m19 to 5m07) the same way. During each stall the archive writes fell under 10 MiB/s. The target `vzdump-sda` is a thin LV on `sda`, a DRAM-less SATA SSD (SPCC, Phison), and about 15 GiB had been written before the first stall: consistent with its write cache filling (inference, not measured). In snapshot mode a guest write to a block not yet backed up waits until the old block is copied into the backup, so a stalled archive writer stalls the lab VM's own writes for as long as the stall lasts (inference from QEMU's copy-before-write; the host kernel log shows no hung task). VM 100's 250 GiB is 218 GiB of zeros and 31.8 GiB of data (archive 23.6 GB). **So `bwlimit` may not address the measured mechanism**: it lowers the archive write rate, which may keep the SSD out of its slow state, and it lengthens the window. **Backup fleecing does address it** (Proxmox 9.2.2 has it, `--fleecing enabled=1,storage=vmdata`): a guest write's old block goes to a fleecing image on the NVMe pool and the guest never waits on `sda`. `ionice` affects only the compressor for a VM snapshot backup (Proxmox's own option text), and VM 100 has one disk, so there is no scratch disk to exclude. **It happens every night** (the 2026-10-02 investigation): on every day with data (Sep 27, 28, 29, Oct 1, 2) routers log guest disk timeouts (`%IOSXE-3-PLATFORM: ... kernel: ata1.00: cmd ... (timeout)`) and RCU stalls between 08:33 and 08:35 UTC; on Oct 2 r6 logged `rcu_sched kthread starved for 56910 jiffies`, the lab VM's iowait rose from 0.02% to 7.93%, its journal was held about 90 s, and Proxmox's IO pressure reached 25% ("some"). Only on Oct 2 did a device crash. | **[C, functional; sorted 2026-10-01]** A decision for the operator, with the measurement that settles it: limit the job's read rate (`pvesh set /cluster/backup/nmas-nightly --bwlimit 102400`, 100 MiB/s, about 42 minutes for VM 100) and count the in-window transitions the next night; if they fall, the I/O is the mechanism. Every C93 before-and-after comparison excludes 08:30 to 08:50 UTC until then. **2026-10-03, the first night with fleecing (measured read-only at 09:00 UTC, against 2026-10-02):** fleecing was used (`attaching fleecing image vmdata:vm-100-fleece-0`); the archive writer still stalled (149 s and 77 s on VM 100; the sda SSD) but no device stopped answering during either stall, no router logged a disk timeout (Oct 2: 12 kernel lines, `ata1.00 … (timeout)` on r1, r2, r4, r6 and an rcu stall on r6), OSPF adjacency changes fell from 24 to 1, the restart reader saw none (Oct 2: r3's crash), and not-answering transitions fell from 30 to 9. The 9 that remain all fall inside the backup's full-speed reads of zero regions (1.2 to 1.4 GiB/s), as Oct 2's later two outages did; none fell in a stretch read at 600 MiB/s or less; VM 102, the observer, was not the cause. **So the next single change is `bwlimit` at 409600 KiB/s (400 MiB/s)**, which caps what the remaining outages line up with and keeps both VMs inside the window (about 23 minutes); 100 MiB/s would run about 93 minutes | this file, 2026-10-01; 2026-10-03 |

| C6 | **A seeded artefact never learns that its source changed, and nothing can say which copies are stale.** Code creates it once, never overwrites it (correctly: it must not clobber a local edit), and never compares it again. The class, not the instance. Surveyed 2026-09-25, **six kinds**: **(1)** the per-list template library: `_common.j2`, `cisco_ios/base.j2`, `cisco_iosxe/base.j2`, `bindings.yml`. *Measured on `default`:* `_common.j2` equals the shipped version at `4771a4c`, carried in by hand as `c8e07cc`, so its only divergence is P.1's 9 lines; the two `base.j2` are current and unedited. **(2)** NetBox objects NMAS creates with **code-defined** attributes and never updates: the `os_version` custom field (label, type, object types) and 4 tag definitions (colours, including `nmas-managed`). A custom-field failure is logged at **DEBUG** (`netbox_client.py:659`), so a stale definition fails invisibly. **(3)** Settings: the v1 migration seeded every key, and the live file was reseeded with 107 defaults on 2026-09-23, so every key there holds that day's default and a later default change never reaches it. `nmas-settings-diff` shows that a value differs, not why. **(4)** Host installs from `deploy/`: 4 systemd units, the rsyslog conf, the Grafana rules. Nothing compares the installed copy with the repo. **(5)** The launch-patch copies on the clab host (`labs/*/patches`), per-lab by design; the guard checks their call site, not their version. **(6)** The legacy `config_git` `.gitignore`: written once, never topped up. `config_repo`'s `ensure_repo_hygiene()` tops up on every access and is the right shape. **Not in the class:** the NDM config template in NetBox, which is re-asserted every sync (the opposite property: it overwrites a NetBox-side edit, and the modification record now records that). **Can anything answer "which lists run a stale seed"? No, for any of the six.** But for (1) the answer exists in data already held: each list's repo has its seed commit, and hashing the network copy against every shipped version in git history classifies each file as *current*, *stale* (equals an older shipped version), *edited* (equals none) or *edited and stale*. That is how `default` was measured above, with no new record. **Answers BUILT 2026-09-25 for (1) and (2):** `templates_repo.seed_status()` (GET `/templates/seed_status`) classifies each file as current / stale / edited / edited_and_stale from the two git histories, and **edited never counts as a defect**. Its first live run was WRONG and its tests passed: a relative path doubled inside `git -C` and every file read `edited_and_stale`, because every fixture used absolute paths. Fixed, re-measured: `_common.j2` stale at `4771a4c`, both `base.j2` current, which agrees with the independent hash measurement. `netbox_client.seeded_spec_status()` compares the custom field and 8 tag specs with NetBox. Live: 7 current, 2 absent (`mpls`, `eigrp`, which no device runs), none differ. `scripts/nmas-seed-status` reports both. **Still unanswered: (4) host installs, (5) lab launch-patch copies, (6) the legacy `.gitignore`.** (3) is split out as C7, and the DEBUG-logging class found beside (2) is C8. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | this file, 2026-09-25 (P.1) |
| C7 | **The 2026-09-23 settings erasure is permanent in a way it had not been named: every non-empty default is FROZEN too.** The erasure's reseed wrote all 107 keys, so this deployment holds that day's default for every key, and a later change to any default never reaches it. `GUARD_GATING_EMPTY_DEFAULTS` covered the keys that default to empty (a guard that silently refuses). This is the rest of them: **nothing distinguishes a value somebody chose from a default that has since moved, and it is silent in both directions.** A moved default does not arrive, and the frozen value reads as a choice (`origin: file`, and `nmas-settings-diff` lists it as *differs*). It is worse than the guards case because nothing refuses. **The same classifier would answer it.** `settings_schema.DEFAULTS` has a git history, so each written value is either today's default (*current*), a past default and not today's (*frozen at an old default*), or no default ever (*chosen*), with *ambiguous* where a chosen value happens to equal a past default. That is not built. | **[B, later: blocks 7.7; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | this file, 2026-09-25 |
| C9 | **The vIOS clock runs at about 75% speed, and reads ~16 hours behind.** Measured on s4, 2026-09-25: 300.0 s of device time is 391–404 s of real time over five intervals. So **every timestamp a switch writes is wrong**: in its syslog lines, its `show logging`, and its captured configs' `Last configuration change`. NTP is configured (`ntp server 10.255.1.10`) and is plainly not keeping s4's clock. Whether NMAS's host serves NTP, and whether any slewing could correct a 25% rate error, is not measured. The heartbeat is safe because its window is per platform; **anything that compares a switch's own timestamps with wall time is not.** **The fix is in P.9 (2026-09-30):** the monitoring profile carries NTP to every device (decision 4), which corrects the switches' clocks; measured drift by then was days (s3's heartbeat stamped Sep 26 on Sep 29). **The rate, measured over the fleet (2026-09-30, `deriv(sysUpTime[1h])/100`):** s1 and s2 0.94, s4 0.76, s3 0.58, the routers 1.00. **Its measured explanation is in C93.** NTP stays in the profile regardless: it keeps the time of day in log lines right, never the rate (`sysUpTime` counts the device's own ticks); at an error of 6 to 42% NTP cannot slew (its limit is about 500 ppm) and can only step, which is what this row's measurement confirms once the profile applies it. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** **DECIDED 2026-09-27 (the operator):** MEASURE whether NTP can correct a 25% rate error. If it cannot, switch timestamps are never compared with wall time, and that is said wherever a timestamp is drawn. (was: decide, then build) | NSOT_PLAN.md P.1 (step 4, s4) |
| C10 | **A batch deploy's circuit breaker counts VERIFY failures and not PUSH failures.** `run_batch()` records a failure towards the breaker only when `outcome == failed and stage == "verify"` (`deploy.py`). A push rejected on every device, such as a command an image does not accept, is therefore attempted and rolled back on **every** device in the batch, never stopped. The breaker's own docstring says repeated failures mean "something systemic… and continuing turns one mistake into nine", and a platform-wide push rejection is the most systemic failure there is. Named while answering whether the P.1 six could be one batch (2026-09-25). The risk there is low, because s4 and r2 had proven both platforms first. **Re-read 2026-10-03 (surveying the code for Coverage's combined deploy):** still so (`modules/nsot/deploy.py`, `run_batch`: only `FAILED` at stage `verify` counts), the limit is `deploy_verify_failure_limit`, default 2, and a verify that DID NOT PASS without raising (intent unmet, unreadable after the change, a quick read-back missing a line) ends `deployed` and is not counted either. The combined deploy's signed board stops at the first failure, of any kind. | **[B, later: blocks 7.4's batch deploy (and protects today's); sorted 2026-09-28]** build | this file, 2026-09-25 |
| C13 | **Syslog lines name a device only if the NMAS host's hand-kept `/etc/hosts` knows it.** Measured 2026-09-25: `/etc/hosts` (last modified 2026-09-07) lists r1–r5 and s1–s4 and not r6, so rsyslog's reverse lookup writes `10.255.1.16` in the hostname field of r6's lines where every other device gets its name. The heartbeat rules are **unaffected by design and pinned**: they key on the device's own `origin-id` field (`\s<host>:\s`), never rsyslog's. Anything keyed on the rsyslog field, such as a Grafana panel or a Loki label, would treat r6 differently, silently. The same shape as `ROUTERS="r1 r2 r3 r4 r5"`: a list complete when written and wrong for every device since. Fix by not depending on it, or have onboarding own the entry. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** **DECIDED 2026-09-27 (the operator):** stop depending on /etc/hosts; key everything on the device's own origin-id. Onboarding owning the entry would make the tool a second owner of one fact in a host file it does not otherwise manage. (was: decide) | NSOT_PLAN.md P.1 (r6's first heartbeat) |

| C78 | **"Re-read at apply" re-reads the STORED capture, never the device, and no stage compares the device with what was confirmed** (found preparing C70, 2026-09-27). `/deploy/apply` and the restore apply set `fresh_captures[hostname] = captured`, the golden read from disk again, so the capture-hash check catches a golden that moved between plan and apply and nothing else. The pipeline reads the device at its pre-change snapshot (stage 4) and compares it with nothing. So a device changed by hand since its golden gets a program computed against a stale capture: merge-only may omit a line the device has lost, and a restore at HEAD of a drifted device sends nothing by construction (target and capture are the same file). The code's own comment says so; **the screens did not**. The deploy wizard said "Each device is re-read at deploy time", and the preview gate this session wrote said "re-read at apply; a changed device is skipped". **The false sentences are fixed** (the gate is "capture unchanged since this preview" and says the device is not compared; pinned). **Open: whether to compare.** Stage 4's running config against the confirmed capture, section-aware (`configs_equivalent`), skipping as drifted on a difference, would make the sentence the old one claimed true. Its cost is what the drift checker learned: a C8000v regenerates its self-signed certificate, so the comparison needs the same normalisation or it refuses every C8000v deploy. | **[UNKNOWN: does anything write the devices outside the pipeline? (E6's device-side log, hours); sorted 2026-09-28]** build, not now (the operator, 2026-09-27: "the device changed since you confirmed" is the one thing a confirm hash cannot protect against on its own). **Its dependency, corrected by measurement:** the certificate normalisation already EXISTS, since `configs_equivalent()` applies `strip_self_signed_certs()`. What must come first is measuring the comparison against the live fleet: each device's running config against its golden, reading equivalent on every device. | C60 | **The deploy RECEIPT is built (7.1, 2026-09-27); the FOLLOW-UP WINDOW is not.** Built: `modules/nsot/receipts.py`. One row per device per batch, written by both apply paths (deploy and restore) after the golden commit, into `lists/<slug>/deploy_receipts.jsonl` at `0600`. Each row holds: the program SENT (masked on the way in) and its hash, compared with the confirmed hash; the verified actor; the checks that RAN (the protocols verify compared, their before and after counts, routes and interfaces), or why verify did not run (refused, nothing to change, stopped at a stage); r6's "no routing protocol" as a real state; rollback; and the golden commit. The commit carries a `Program-Hash:` trailer per device. A device refused before sending gets a row saying so. The result screen draws what was checked per device and the receipt's outcome, and a receipt that fails to write is drawn in red and logged at ERROR, never turning a deploy that happened into one that reads as failed. The storage checker classifies the file as no-secret and SCANS it. Every row states `follow_up: not_run` and `second_reading: not_built`, rather than leaving the keys out. **Still open, the second half:** the follow-up window. A deploy is finished when nothing has complained for N minutes. A declared job closes each row with what fired on the device and its neighbours and WHAT WAS WATCHING; "quiet, watched by: …", never "fine"; a row whose alert source was not read says so. It needs 7.2's alert reader, and C58 to reach an open page. Found while building: the payload check's own `/deploy/apply` provider planned without the authorisation it applied with, so its "deployed row" had always been a refusal, and a deployed row's keys were never examined. Fixed: the provider exhibits both. | **[B, later: blocks Stage 8 (8.6 to 8.8); sorted 2026-09-28]** build (the follow-up window: a job behind 7.1) | [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md) 8.6, 8.7, 8.8 |
| C93 | **s3, the manager's gateway, forwards for the whole fleet while failing to answer for itself** (found inside C92's measurement; the operator's emphasis, 2026-09-27). Every other device's traffic to the NMAS crosses s3's `Vlan99`, and in 106 hours of the host's ping log s3 missed a probe 1,540 times, about one every four minutes; 1,362 of its 1,478 single-probe misses came with no other device down. So its data plane worked while its control plane did not answer ICMP or TCP:22 within 2 s. It is its own row, not a line in C92, because it is not about the dot: s3 is the one device where a surprise costs the whole lab, and a measured responsiveness problem on it had been seen by nothing. **The clock correlation** (s3's clock runs at 0.55 to 0.61, s4's at 0.75, and they miss most: 1,540 and 227 against 161 to 192 for every other device) is suggestive, not a cause. **It predicts something testable**: if a starved emulator is the cause, s3's misses track the clab VM's load (which sat at 15 or more overnight). **Measured the same day, the competing explanation is ruled out**: the PROBER is not starved. The NMAS host's load at s3's misses (median 0.56, p90 1.04) matches its load over the whole span (0.55, 1.06), and s3's misses per hour against the host's hourly load give a Spearman of 0.07 over 108 hours. **The prediction itself cannot be tested from stored data**: Prometheus's node exporter covers the NMAS host only (`localhost:9100`), so the clab VM's load has no history anywhere this tool can read. **Also open, not filed: the 92-minute s3 outage at 2026-09-27 02:34**, during the ZTP work. The transition log cannot tell "s3 was down" from "the app stopped", which is itself the finding: a reader that logs only transitions cannot distinguish its own absence from its subject's. **The misses are stalls, not slowness** (the operator's arithmetic, 2026-09-27). r2 answers in 23 to 31 ms across s3's Vlan99, and the probe gives up only after 2 s: about 65 to 85 times the normal round trip. On the host the probe is TCP:22 alone (C92: `ping3` cannot open a socket there). **Corrected the same day (C97):** a miss is EITHER 2 s with no answer OR an instant refusal, and the second is what r2 did when every vty line was held by the tool's own leaked sessions. A refused connect returns in milliseconds, so it is not a stall at all. The probe records neither kind, so "s3 has multi-second stalls" is one of two readings of its 1,540 misses, not a finding. s3 has sixteen vty lines, which makes exhaustion harder than on a five-line router, not impossible. Either way it matters beyond the dot, because every SSH session the tool opens to s3 meets whichever it is. **The after-measurement (2026-10-01, 13:05 UTC, read-only from Prometheus and the app log):** six clean hourly windows before the changes (ending 03:00 to 08:00) against four after (ending 10:00 to 13:00); the window ending 09:00 is excluded (the backup at 08:30 and the changes at 08:34 to 08:41). Clock rate: s3 0.527 to 0.557 (+5.8%), the controls s1 +0.5%, s2 +0.7%, s4 +2.8%, so every switch sped up a little (the host's contention fell) and s3 most; still below its own 0.565 to 0.573 the evening before. s3's output discards 1.78 to 1.17 pps (-34%), still 3 to 5 times the evening before (0.24 to 0.39); no errors on any switch. Failed scrapes as a fraction (the interval doubled, so counts do not compare): s3 2.1% to 0.6%, s1 1.6% to 0.4%, s4 0.5% to 0.3%. The longest scrape still reaches the 30 s timeout on s3 every hour, where the controls now mostly stay under it. Not-answering transitions (the reachability reader): 8 from 00:00 to 08:00 (3 of them s3's), 1 from 09:00 to 13:05 (r1), none on s3. The operator raised that job's `scrape_timeout` to 55 s (interval 60 s; read back as loaded); the walk then took s4 5.6 s, s1 10.6 s, s2 11.9 s and s3 14.1 s, about half s3's time before the reboot (docs/PROMETHEUS_TARGETS.md). The device-unreachable alert fired on that walk's `up` while s3 answered SNMP: recorded in NSOT_PLAN P.7 (unreachable rests on a light signal; a slow poll is its own, lower rule). **The trajectory from the reboot is being measured** (read-only, 15-minute and hourly windows of s3's clock rate, discards and walk duration against s1, s2 and s4): if s3 starts fast and degrades over hours something accumulates and a periodic planned reload becomes an option; if it holds, the overnight reductions were enough. The windows containing the restart (15:30, 15:45) are artefacts and are not read as rates. **2026-10-02, the operator's four switch "restarts" were not restarts** (measured read-only for the operator: Prometheus, Loki, the lab VM and Proxmox; checked again directly, `resets(sysUpTime{s1..s4}[24h])` = 0 at 18:09 UTC). The times (s1 18:23, s2 18:26, s4 22:42 on Oct 1, s3 03:50 on Oct 2) are wall-clock time minus each switch's uptime, and the vIOS clocks run slow, so the uptime does too: measured two ways (sysUpTime's gain per wall second, and the heartbeats' device time against Loki's receive time), s1 0.943, s2 0.941, s3 0.559, s4 0.767, the routers 1.000. Every switch booted once, in the lab redeploy (destroy 16:55, deploy 16:57:56, qemu start about 16:58 UTC Oct 1), which is also r1, r2 and r4's "1 day 35 min"; s3 also at 15:24 Oct 1 (a stop and start through the containerlab API). sysUpTime and the switches' syslog sequence numbers run unbroken through all four stated times, heartbeats arrive throughout, and nothing on the lab VM, Proxmox or NMAS stands out then. "Unknown reason" is probably what vIOS reports after a fresh qemu power-on (inference; no `show version` was read). Each switch's qemu holds one host CPU at 75 to 105%, s3 the busiest and the slowest clock (inference: a saturated single vCPU slows the guest's clock; not tested). **s3's container stopping at 16:43:27 UTC Oct 1 was vrnetlab's launch script crashing** (the operator, read before the redeploy replaced the container, so the log is gone): `docker ps -a` showed "Exited (1)" and its log ended in `scrapli.exceptions.ScrapliTimeout: timed out reading from transport` (raised from scrapli/decorators.py `_handle_timeout`). It took every device's management path down until 16:51. **The lesson (the operator's): never derive an event's time from a device's uptime on these platforms.** The vIOS clocks run slow, so now-minus-uptime placed one redeploy as four reboots hours apart; use the uptime counter RESETTING (the restarts reader, C341) and the device's syslog (sequence numbers, the receive time). | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28; its UNKNOWN answered 2026-09-30: not the lab host, s3's own single vCPU (below)]** **Measured on the lab host (the operator, 2026-09-30):** every node runs `-enable-kvm -cpu host`, the switches `-smp 1` and the routers `-smp 2`; `docker stats` shows each switch pegging its single vCPU (89 to 108%) and r1 at 218%; `vmstat` shows steal 0 throughout, the lab VM 35 to 39% idle and about 51 to 56% guest time. So the clock is **not** software emulation, **not** Proxmox starving the VM, and **not** the lab VM out of cores. **The explanation, most likely and not yet shown directly: nested virtualisation makes timer-interrupt delivery expensive.** Classic IOS keeps time by COUNTING those interrupts, so a switch whose vCPU is busy loses the ones it cannot take; IOS-XE uses a paravirtual clock and keeps time (1.00). s3 loses most because it is the busiest (it forwards the fleet's management traffic on the vCPU that also runs its control plane). **Staged run 7** (NSOT_STAGE7_PLAN) tests it on a throwaway vIOS: re-delivering lost ticks, one QEMU option per boot, the rate before and after under one load. **The alert tier (NSOT_PLAN P.7):** a protocol drops below hello/dead (OSPF 0.25), so s3 alerts only below about 0.38; at 0.58 it is the first CHRONIC entry, folded once on Needs attention and linked here, and amber on the Overview. Nothing changes on s3 outside a planned redeploy. The after-measurement (the same queries, windows ending 10:00 onward, the change window excluded) is recorded here the following morning. **s3's heartbeat across its reboot** (the operator's question after the 16:03Z check read "STALE RATE s3 ... gaps are now 515.1-757.1 s"; read-only from Loki and Prometheus at 16:40Z). The 757.1 s gap is THE REBOOT, not a clock at 40%: the last heartbeat before it arrived 15:20:35, the first after 15:33:12, and s3's own sysUpTime (402.91 s at the 15:36:00 sample) puts the boot no later than 15:29:17Z, about 09:29 local. The 515.1 s gap is BEFORE the reboot (the 13:00Z hour). Split at the reboot over 24 h: before, 148 gaps, median 540.8 s (rate 0.555), shortest 474.8 s; after, 7 gaps, median 541.7 s (rate 0.554), longest 569.0 s. **The reboot changed nothing about s3's clock.** Hour by hour the heartbeat rate was 0.567 to 0.603 the evening before, fell to 0.516 to 0.544 overnight (00:00 to 08:00Z), and stepped to 0.555 to 0.564 at 09:00Z, six and a half hours BEFORE the reboot, the hour the operator's 08:30 to 08:55Z changes took effect; the Prometheus clock rate showed the same step (0.527 to 0.557). So what slows s3 is still there, and the reboot did not reset it. The false STALE itself is C299: the measurement counted the boot gap as an interval. **Over 7 days** (read 2026-10-01 for P.7's decided lookback, restarts excluded: two for s3, this morning's and the afternoon's redeploy): 991 gaps, median rate 0.583, 0.5th to 99.5th percentile 472.7 to 609.0 s, extremes 412.4 and 639.8 s, and every extreme in the nightly backup window (08:35 to 09:01 UTC, C289), the shortest a catch-up burst after a stall. s1 showed the same shape (261.0 to 393.1 s extremes around a 316 to 331 s band): the backup window's contention reaches the switches' heartbeats, not only s3's. **s3's container exiting (2026-10-01 afternoon), from vrnetlab's vIOS launch script read read-only on the lab host:** at each container start, once the console shows the prompt, `apply_config()` types the whole startup config through scrapli and sends `write memory`, every operation bounded by `SCRAPLI_TIMEOUT` (default 900 s, read from the container's environment, so settable per kind or per node with containerlab's `env:`; this lab's `cisco_vios` kind already carries an `env:` block). A `ScrapliTimeout` there is caught by nothing: `VM.work()` catches only `EOFError` (a dropped console, which restarts the VM inside the container), so the exception leaves `VR.start()`'s loop, the launch script dies, and the container exits. Its restart policy is `no`, and a `docker start` would not rebuild containerlab's links in any case, so the node stays down until a redeploy. Other paths: no console output for 300 spins restarts the VM inside the container; qemu writing to stderr restarts it; a guest that reloads inside a running VM is never seen by vrnetlab (it boots NVRAM, which `apply_config` saved). Read, not measured: a qemu that exits writing nothing to stderr appears to leave the node reported healthy with no VM. s3 had already waited the full 900 s, so a longer bound is not the fix: the cause is the lab host's CPU contention (C289's backup window among it). **Recommended, for the operator (a lab-host write):** a patched vIOS launch script bound per node like the c8000v one, catching the timeout in `apply_config()` and retrying the boot rather than exiting; and reducing contention first, since a patch only turns a dead node into a slow one. **The evening's trajectory** (Prometheus, read-only, 12:00 to 20:30 UTC in 30-min steps; the 19:40 read's timer died with its session, so it was taken at 20:45): s3's sysUpTime puts its own restart at about 15:40 and the redeploy's at about 17:09. Its clock rate after both is 0.551 to 0.568, the band it held before them (0.550 to 0.573 from 12:00 to 15:00), so neither restart changed what slows it. The 17:00 step reads every switch at 0.24 to 0.43, which is the redeploy's own boot inside the 30-min window, not a measurement. s3 alone keeps discarding output (0.4 to 2.5 pps; s1, s2 and s4 zero), its SNMP scrapes average 7.5 to 9.3 s against the others' 4.9 to 8.3, peaking at 41 to 45 s in the hour after the redeploy, and no switch has missed a scrape since 18:00. **The night's read, with the output-discards alert** (the operator: "fired again tonight, chronic overload"; Prometheus and the app's stored Grafana reader, read-only via LAN, 2026-10-02 00:45Z, 30-min windows from 20:00Z): the alert is firing since 00:43:50Z for `ifDescr=GigabitEthernet1/1` on `instance=10.255.1.23`, s3, named by its address (the same labelling as "Interface down", P.7's note). **The discards are almost all on ONE port, Gi1/1, the NMAS-facing one** ("NMAS via br-mgmt", VLAN 99): 0.70 to 2.79 pps there against 0.12 to 0.31 on Gi1/0 (r1's core link), so the egress that drops is toward the manager: SNMP replies, syslog, telemetry, Oxidized fetches and the tool's own reads. The highest window, 00:00Z (3.1 pps in all, 2.8 on Gi1/1), held Save All's nine reads (23:52Z) and the clab sync (00:02Z): a correlation, not a measured cause. s3's clock rate 0.554 to 0.566 throughout (the band it has held since the morning's changes; s1, s2 0.94, s4 0.76 to 0.78), its scrapes 7.5 to 9.8 s on average against the others' 4.9 to 8.2, peaking at 43.7 s, and no failed scrape on any switch. Unchanged in kind from the evening: chronic, steady, on the manager's port. C314 (2026-10-01) adds one Oxidized fetch per changed device after each golden commit, which crosses this port. |
| C94 | **The lab host is unmonitored: the machine running every device in the lab has no metrics collected at all** (the operator, 2026-09-27, from C93's measurement). Prometheus's node exporter covers the NMAS host only (`localhost:9100`). The clab VM, 24 vCPUs, at load 15 or more all night, with each C8000v burning about two cores while idle, has no history this tool or anyone can read. That is why C93 cannot be tested, and why any later "the lab is slow" question could not be either. It is its own row, separate from C93, because the gap is the host and not s3. **What to collect, and why each** (proposed for the operator, who runs the change on a shared machine):<br>- **per-container CPU** (cAdvisor, scraped by Prometheus): the direct test of C93, since the hypothesis is that s3's CONTAINER is starved rather than the host being busy. vrnetlab runs each device's qemu inside its container, so the container's CPU is the device's;<br>- **CPU pressure** (PSI, `node_pressure_cpu_waiting_seconds_total`, in the node exporter since 1.x): time runnable tasks spent waiting for a CPU. It measures starvation itself, where load only counts queue length;<br>- **CPU steal** (`node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="steal"}`): the VM waiting on Proxmox, a starvation the VM's own load cannot show;<br>- **per-container throttling** (`container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total`), if any container has a CPU limit;<br>- load, CPU and memory (with swap and memory PSI), and **disk I/O pressure**, since a C8000v boot and `write memory` are disk-bound;<br>- clocks: the lab host and the NMAS host on NTP (chrony), with the offset recorded, since correlating s3's misses with the lab host's metrics compares two machines' timestamps.<br>Scrape at 15 s: a miss is one 5 s probe, and a 15 s rate of PSI or container CPU resolves it. Retention to match the NMAS host's 90 days.<br>**C198's remaining question lives here** (2026-09-29): `show running-config` took 2 to 10 times longer on the same device three minutes apart (s4 6.0 against 16.6 s, r1 0.6 against 6.4 s) while connects held steady, so the variance is device-side execution; the lab host's load is the leading candidate and has no history to test it against. **Partly measured by hand (the operator, 2026-09-30, for C93):** a snapshot of `docker stats` and `vmstat` during normal running: steal 0, the lab VM 35 to 39% idle, each switch's single vCPU pegged. That answered C93's host question without collected history, and is why the collection proposed here is for the NEXT question (load over time, per container), not C93's. | **[C, M (C93's measurement); sorted 2026-09-28]** the operator runs it (a change on a shared machine); then C93's correlation | this file, 2026-09-27 |

Closing 6.1 changes what reads Oxidized and NetBox (NMAS reads NetBox at the LAN address), which the rotation's consumer work must also match. **Remedy BUILT 2026-09-28, the write is the operator's:** `scripts/nmas-netbox-mask-context` masks what NetBox HOLDS: the population is the checker's own scan, the authority is the modification record (did NMAS write this context; any other is named and left alone), the masking is the import's own `masked_context()`, a partly masked context is refused (the redactor is not idempotent), and the result is read back with the checker's scan. Dry run on the live NetBox: r5 only, 3 lines and 1 structured value, authority 06:17:34, and the masked result passes the scan with every other key unchanged. **Open, for the operator:** (a) whether `nmas-retire` masks the NetBox context as part of the exit (it keeps the record; the record need not keep the secret), so the next retire does not recreate this; (b) rotating the fleet's community, since masking does not undo exposure (C95's P.2 backups hold it for their retention either way) **MASKED 2026-09-28 (the operator): r5 dry run, then apply, read back clean, 11 context keys untouched, authority from the modification record** (C95 closed on it). **DECIDED 2026-09-28 (the operator):** (1) **the community is the finding, and it is ROTATED**: readable in NetBox to every account and token, in P.2's backups for their retention, and `public`, the value anyone tries first, so both facts are fixed at once rather than a guessable secret restored. Through intent and the deploy path, like P.1's block. **C79 is its prerequisite**: after the first community rotation the old value is a DIFFERENT line, so a restore adds it beside the new one and nothing refuses; the rotation is what populates C79's class. Order: C79, then the rotation. (2) **Retire masks the NetBox record as part of the exit**, built with retire's other work in 7.3 (the import used the LIST as a stand-in for "everything NMAS wrote into NetBox", the proxy-population rule in the import's own scope). It changes what retire does to a SHARED system, so retire's preview must say so.  **Found planning the rotation, 2026-09-28, before anything moved:** (a) **through the deploy path the rotation is ADD-ONLY** (C12's class, its second member): merge-only never negates, so new intent adds the new `snmp-server community` line and leaves the old value (`public`) enabled beside it. The rotation finishes only with a removal: the device's own old line negated VERBATIM, as onboarding already removes the RW community (a rebuilt line drops the ACL and does not match). That removal needs a path the deploy does not have. (b) **Every consumer moves in the same window, or monitoring goes dark**: in the repo, `collector_config.get_snmp_community()` and `snmp_collector.get_device_summary(..., community="public")`, whose hard-coded default is the old value; outside it, the Prometheus SNMP exporter's config on the host (not yet measured). Decide the removal path and list the consumers before rotating.  **DECIDED 2026-09-28 (the operator):** removal by extending onboarding's verbatim removal into a rotation operation (it removes the exact line it read, never a synthesised `no`, the discipline of `rollback_commands()`, leaving merge-only intact; no Mode B), which reads, removes, then READS BACK (the persist/C112 rule); add and remove ONE operation per device; consumers measured first, all changed to read the new value from ONE place, then the devices; s4 first. **Consumers MEASURED on the host 2026-09-28 (read-only, by hash):** (1) the SNMP exporter (docker `prom/snmp-exporter:v0.29.0`, :9116) reads `/etc/snmp_exporter/snmp.yml`, whose auths `public_v1`/`public_v2` hold the fleet value; four Prometheus jobs use `public_v2` (cisco_8000v 5 targets, cisco_vios_l2 4, cisco_ipsla 5, lldp 9); the auth's NAME states the value. (3) **NMAS's own SNMP reads no configured value at all**: no list has a `collector_config.json`, so `get_snmp_community()` returns its literal fallback, and `snmp_get`/`snmp_walk`/`get_device_summary` default to the same literal; it works only because the fleet's value EQUALS the code's default (measured: equal). A secret whose owner is a default argument: one owner per fact, like `tftp_server_ip` (C48). (4) The value is held per device in the credential store as `default:<host>:snmp_community_ro`, nine copies, r5's included (a retired device's secret still stored). (5) `/etc/prometheus/snmp.yml` holds it and NOTHING reads it (the apt exporter unit is inactive; the container mounts `/etc/snmp_exporter`): a copy of a secret in a dead file. (6) **Not consumers, measured:** Telegraf (active inputs are host metrics and MDT on :57000, no SNMP), Oxidized (SSH), and the host's own `snmpd` (its own agent; its config is unreadable to this account, so "not the fleet's value" is UNPROVEN, not shown).  **The survey completed 2026-09-28 (the operator: the list must be complete):** (7) the host's own `snmpd` ANSWERS the fleet's value (v1 and v2c) on localhost only and refuses a random one (the control), measured by asking it since its config is unreadable to this account; none of Prometheus's four SNMP jobs targets it, so it is not a consumer of the devices' community: the same value, as an install default, on its own agent. Its own decision, named so the list is complete. (8) The literal: introduced once (`beec347`, 2026-04-10) and never anything but the fleet's value; see C141 for the repository. **DECIDED (the operator): the file_sd / per-device-secret design.** The transitional state is what a per-device rotation IS, so every consumer holds the value per device: NMAS reads each device's own secret (the credential store intent renders from) and the literal defaults go; Prometheus's SNMP targets move to a `file_sd` file NMAS writes, each target labelled with its auth; the exporter's `snmp.yml` and `snmptrapd.conf` hold one entry per value in use, written by a host script run with sudo that writes, reloads, READS BACK and says what the service now holds. Only a device whose intent is absent or only the bootstrap is seeded: over full intent it would replace what the device SHOULD be with what it IS, so that device is shown, not selectable, and the apply checks again (the hash cannot see intent committed in between; a control removing that check was caught only by a test added for it). | C174 | **NetBox scoping: tenants against regions, and per-list containers** (the operator, 2026-09-28: deferred, recorded with both sides). **The case for tenants (the operator):** a tenant means "who this belongs to", which is what a list is; regions mean geography and should stay for geography; every object carries a tenant field natively; and two lists could each have a site called "HQ", which region-named sites cannot hold. NetBox filters by tenant throughout its UI, so "this network's objects" is one click. Regions-as-lists spends the geographic hierarchy on something that is not geography, so a list whose devices are genuinely in two places cannot say so. And the measurement below makes the move cheap either way, which the operator named as the better reason to defer than any argument about which model is right. **The migration's questions, measured:** the sync writes one region and one site per list, both named after the list (`_ensure_region`, `_ensure_site`); the provenance record keys on object ids per type, `dcim/regions` included, and removal's order deletes regions; nothing else reads the region (only `netbox_client.py` and `netbox_guard.py` name it). Placed in Stage 9, not the end of Stage 7: P.8 adds the per-list `netbox_scope` key that every writer reads, so after P.8 a tenant move changes a value and the scope reader, not every writer. P.8 makes it cheaper, so it waits without its cost growing. Regions work and the model does its job | `modules/netbox_client.py`; NSOT_PLAN P.8 |

| C322 | **The Heartbeat tab's click looked like it did nothing: its page measured inside the GET, 7.5 s, with nothing drawn busy** (the operator, 2026-10-02: "clicking the Heartbeat tab did NOTHING"). Measured on the host, read-only, every v2 tab and page: all answer 200. The Heartbeat page (`routes/v2.heartbeat` -> `heartbeat_windows.plan()`) reads every device's 7 days of Loki arrivals and Prometheus's restarts before it answers, 7.5 s on loopback, longer through the tunnel. Monitoring's tabs are plain page links, so nothing moves on the page while the browser waits (the device page's tabs load through htmx and dim their body; the slowest, r3's Logs, 4.8 s, is drawn busy). The next slowest pages: IP SLA 0.9 s, Coverage 0.7 s, every other v2 page under 0.3 s, and no device-page fragment over 2 s. The rule it breaks: a control that starts work shows it is busy ON ITSELF (NSOT_GUI_BRIEF section 6). The tab is removed (the operator's decision, the same turn), so this click is gone; the screen remains, reached from Needs attention, and still measures in its GET. **Wanted (the operator's mockup review, 2026-10-02, replacing the first answer):** NO page. The hourly heartbeat check re-measures each window from the 7-day spread and keeps the installed rules current with nobody involved; a person hears of the heartbeat only from the alert when a device's heartbeat stops. The page and its routes are removed when that lands. The privileged install (the root-owned provisioning file and the Grafana restart) goes through a root-owned path unit like the updater's (AGREED by the operator the same day), which validates the file against the GENERATOR'S OWN SCHEMA before installing it and restarting Grafana: only generated heartbeat rules, for managed devices, in the expected folder and group; never a looser "looks like heartbeat rules". | **[C, functional; sorted 2026-10-02]** the automatic re-measure and its root-owned install (design agreed 2026-10-02) | `routes/v2.py` `_heartbeat_ctx`, `modules/heartbeat_windows.py` |

| C427 | **Nothing checks that a device holds the ROLE its intent designs, only that its protocols are up** (the operator, 2026-10-04, from s3's page; the register searched first: E7 built golden state as every declared protocol FULL or 2WAY, which this passes). On VLAN 100 s3 is FULL with r2 and r4 and 2WAY with r1, r3 and s4, so r2 and r4 are DR and BDR; r3's intent sets `ip ospf priority 255`, the designed DR. DR election does not preempt, so after r3's crash it stayed DROTHER. Nothing is broken; the network is not in its intended state and nothing says so. | **[C, functional; sorted 2026-10-04: forwarding works, the designed roles do not hold]** under E7's "configured AND working": an operational check of intended roles against what is running (the DR and BDR a segment's priorities design, BGP's best path where intent prefers one), a Needs attention row naming the role, who holds it and how it is restored (for OSPF a disruptive `clear ip ospf process` on the holders, the operator's to choose); golden state's claim says which it checked. **Recorded 2026-10-04 (the away queue's item 7, not built). Does anything need r3 as DR? Measured read-only on every committed golden on the host: no.** The VLAN 100 segment's priorities are r3 255, r4 200, r1 and r2 100, s3 and s4 0 (so the design is r3 DR, r4 BDR). Nothing in any golden refers to who holds the role: no `track` on OSPF, no first-hop redundancy on that segment (s1 and s2's VRRP is on their own VLANs 10 to 30, tracking an interface), and each IP SLA is a probe, not a condition. The DR only originates the segment's network LSA and relays its flooding; routes and costs are the same whoever holds it. So the gap is intent versus state, not forwarding. **The check's design:** E7's working-state judge gains a ROLES claim beside PROTOCOLS: for each multi-access segment in intent, the DR and BDR its priorities design (highest priority, ties broken by router ID; priority 0 never) against what `ospfNbrState`/the interface table report from Prometheus (no device session); a mismatch is a warning row naming the segment, the designed and the actual holders, and the restore (`clear ip ospf process` on the holders, disruptive, the operator's to choose, so the row offers it as a previewed operation and never runs it); golden state's claim then says "protocols up, roles as designed" or "protocols up, roles not checked". BGP's preferred path is the same shape and waits for intent that declares a preference. **DECIDED 2026-10-04 (the operator): leave the network as it is. The ROLES claim REPORTS as information with a choice, never a Warning.** For example, "r3 is intended as DR (priority 255); r2 holds the role". The row is at information level, with two actions: accept (an acknowledgement that clears it), or restore at a convenient moment (the previewed operation, the operator's to run). The design above changes accordingly: "a warning row" reads "an information row with a choice" | `modules/nsot/golden_state.py`, verify |

Whether s4's "CSCI 5840 Lab 4" is intended is the operator's **DECIDED (the operator, 2026-10-04): change, never remove; no Mode B.** r2, s3, s4 Loopback0 -> "mgmt identity" (as r3's); s3 GigabitEthernet0/1 and s4 GigabitEthernet0/1 -> "spare" (no link in the topology; s4's "CSCI 5840 Lab 4" not kept). **CHECK BUILT 2026-10-04:** authored intent may not carry a description that is a run's notes (the tool's "NSoT-managed - " prefix, "smoke", "check", a course code; `hostvars.SESSION_DESCRIPTIONS`), refused by the intent editor with the line, by its commit and by bulk intent per device; extracted intent and a restore's record are not judged. **THE EDITS ARE THE OPERATOR'S** (the intent lives in the list's config repo on the host, and host writes are the operator's; its remote holds plaintext secrets and a push there would make the host's own pushes fail): in today's intent editor (the v2 page's Edit intent), ONE save per device, each a commit: r2.yml line 33 (Loopback0) `description: mgmt identity`; s3.yml line 23 (Loopback0) `description: mgmt identity` and line 84 (GigabitEthernet0/1) `description: spare`; s4.yml line 23 (Loopback0) `description: mgmt identity` and line 84 (GigabitEthernet0/1) `description: spare` (line numbers read on the host 2026-10-04). | C431 | **`test_scale`'s read bound failed once under the gate's load** (found 2026-10-04: the gate for R12/R14 refused when `test_reading_900_devices_is_fast` measured 79.6 ms against its 50 ms bound, with the three shards running at once; it passed three of three alone, at about 0.3 s a run including startup; the register searched first). The bound is not derived from a measurement under the load it runs in (the bounds rule: 2.5x a measured time, written beside it). | **[C, functional; sorted 2026-10-04: a test that refuses gates at random]** measure the read under the gate's three-shard load, set the bound at 2.5x that, written beside it | `tests/test_scale.py` |
| C435 | **Another person's deploy finishing re-ticks every device a person unticked on Coverage** (found 2026-10-04 surveying R27's broadcast half; the register searched first: C377 is Coverage's disabled boxes, a different defect). Read from the code, not yet seen in a browser: the deploy job announces `goldens` and `intent` to EVERY page (`modules/invalidation.py`, `"deploy-job"`), Coverage's whole section re-renders on both (`templates/v2/_coverage.html`, `hx-swap="outerHTML"`, no `hx-include`), and the re-render ticks every offered row (`monitoring_coverage`, `row["checked"] = True`). So a person's unticks silently revert while they choose. The apply preview still names each device before anything is sent, so nothing is sent unseen, but the grid shows a selection the person did not make. R27's broadcast half would make it happen on every write. | **[C, functional; sorted 2026-10-04: the preview names every device before a send; the grid looks right and is not]** the one measurement first: in a real browser, untick a row, announce `goldens`, and see the box come back. The fix: a subscriber that holds a person's input keeps it across a re-render (the selection sent with `hx-include`, or the swap skipped while the form is dirty). R27's broadcast half waits for this | `templates/v2/_coverage.html`, `modules/monitoring_coverage.py`, R27 |

| E2 | **Q3 — empty-command rejections at boot.** r3–r5 each logged two rejections of an empty command during the redeploy; the source of the blank line is unfound. Explicitly **not** to be silenced by filtering the log. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | NSOT_PLAN.md, *Queued from the Stage 2 session* |
| E3 | **Tighten `transport input` on r1–r5.** They still carry `transport input all`. An ordinary authored-intent deploy, deliberately done one device at a time, scheduled after r6's branch site — which is now done. **Extended 2026-10-04 (the operator's note from s3; every switch golden read on the host, read-only):** s1 to s4 run `transport input telnet ssh` on `line vty 0 4` and `5 15`; the same change covers them (NSOT_PLAN Stage 9, "The switches, read 2026-10-04"). | **[C, L; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | NSOT_PLAN.md, *Deferred* |
| E5 | **Authorization: roles and separation of duties are DECIDED and unbuilt.** Any verified person may do every gate kind today. The decision covers the roles, the split of `approve` into `author` and `approve`, per-artifact separation of duties, host-side storage, and the `any_person` default. Two parts are needed before any build: measure whether Access tokens carry groups, and D10's trailer. Stage 7 already draws for it (pattern 6). **Attribution now serves two purposes** (the operator, 2026-09-27): D10's `Actor-Verified` and P.3's person-gated confirms were built as security controls, and they are also the foundation of 8.8's written override reasons, an ACCOUNTABILITY control counted per person and per device. An override attributed to "user" or "pipeline" would be worthless. The second purpose is why attribution stays strict where the security argument alone might have relaxed it, and any change to the role model is checked against both. | **[C, L; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | [NSOT_AUTHORIZATION.md](NSOT_AUTHORIZATION.md); NSOT_PLAN.md *AUTHZ* |
| E6 | **Make the DEVICE the witness of its own config changes: `archive` / `log config` / `notify syslog`** (pulled forward by the operator, 2026-09-27, from 8.7's checklist). Measured: RESTCONF on r1-r4 and r6, NETCONF on those five and s1, and no device logs its own config changes, so there are writable paths to the fleet that the pipeline does not own and nothing witnesses. A device-side change log is the only control that closes that gap however the change arrives, and it feeds 8.6 (who changed it) and 8.7 (positive attribution). It is a syslog change, so it goes through INTENT the way P.1's block did. The pipeline exists, and so does P.1b's bulk intent. **Not modelled today**: no parser, template or fixture carries `archive`, so the parser, template and round-trip change comes first (the decision rule: every device, so it is modelled). Then measure arrival per device, with a floor, as the heartbeat was. | **[B, later: blocks 8.7; (L); sorted 2026-09-28]** build | [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md) 8.7 |

| C301 | **After the afternoon's redeploy r3 came back with its vty lines in one stanza (`line vty 0 4`, `length 0` on all five) while r1, r2 and r4 came back in the split form their goldens hold (`line vty 0`, a bare `line vty 1`, `line vty 2 4`), from identical inputs** (the operator, 2026-10-01: the Save All preview showed r1, r2 and r4 differing by certificate only, r3 by certificate and vty). **Read read-only:** vrnetlab's c8000v launch script regenerates `config.iso` at EVERY container start from its own bootstrap (hostname, its `username admin` unless the startup config defines one, `line vty 0 4` with `logging synchronous`, `login local` and `transport input all`, the `clab-mgmt` VRF and Gi1, RESTCONF and NETCONF) followed by the node's `configs/<h>.cfg`, and attaches it with `-cdrom` on every qemu start; IOS-XE's CVAC applies it. A redeploy is a new container from the image's disk, so every redeploy is a first boot for CVAC; whether a reload INSIDE a running VM re-applies it is not measured. The four startup files (`labs/lab/configs/r1..r4.cfg`, harvested 04:34 UTC) hold the SAME vty stanzas byte for byte, the same as the four goldens, and the bootstrap's vty block is the same for all four. So the day-0 content cannot explain why only r3 changed. Loki holds no CVAC or CONFIG line from the boots: the routers send syslog only once their own config's logging lines apply, after CVAC. **What would settle it, a device read (the operator's, with the read-only command box):** on r1 to r4, `show logging | include CVAC|CONFIG_I|CONFIG_P` and `show running-config | section line vty`; the first says whether each applied day-0 and when, the second whether r1, r2 and r4 hold the split form now (the preview compared captures, which may be older than the boot). **Recommendation:** adopt r3's post-boot vty into r3's intent, not deploy intent back: the split form is not something a merge can produce (a bare `line vty 1` sends nothing, and removing `length 0` from four lines is a Mode B shape nobody has measured), every fresh boot of this lab gives CVAC the chance to rewrite it again, and the next Oxidized harvest will write r3's new form into `configs/r3.cfg`, so the lab's own startup file will agree with the adoption. Then Save All for the baseline. If the device read shows r1, r2 and r4 also in one stanza now, adopt all four. **Settled by the operator's read (2026-10-01):** CVAC's own day-0 session leaves `length 0` on whichever vty it ran as (r1, r2, r4 as vty1; r3 this boot as vty0), so the vty state after a redeploy is not deterministic. **Decided:** intent for every IOS-XE router carries one `line vty 0 4` stanza with `length 0`; `deploy/intent-changes/c301-vty-standard.json` (r1 to r4, one group, previewed read-only) and `-r6.json` (r6 keeps `transport input ssh`; its file is hand-formatted, so the editor). Computing it found C307. **The two "'' was rejected" CVAC lines** (r3, r4; read from vrnetlab's launch script, not measured): its bootstrap template interpolates `{ipv6_route}` and `{ipv6_address}`, empty when the node has no IPv6 management address, so `iosxe_config.txt` carries two empty lines and CVAC refuses each. Recorded, not fixed. | **[UNKNOWN: whether r1, r2 and r4 hold the split form after the boot and whether each applied day-0. The measurement: `show logging | include CVAC|CONFIG_I|CONFIG_P` and `show running-config | section line vty` on r1 to r4; sorted 2026-10-01]** functional; the device read decides, then the operator's adoption and Save All | r1 to r4; `labs/lab/configs/` on the lab host; vrnetlab's c8000v launch script |

constraints are in [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md), *Scope of what remains*,
where **Stage 5's count is flagged as a reading of an acceptance paragraph
rather than an enumeration**: it is the one figure there with a different
provenance from the rest, and enumerating Stage 5 is the first item of Stage 5.

**A1 was the one whose absence makes other work untrustworthy** rather than merely incomplete: a mistake in NetBox has nothing
to restore from. It is now P.2, scheduled ahead of Stage 7 for that reason.

C1 was the second such item until it was measured, and the correction is worth
keeping: a finding recorded as a defect became the explanation for the next
confusing output, and that output was correct behaviour. *A pattern that has
been right four times is exactly the one to distrust on the fifth* — the
register makes a finding easier to find, and easier to reach for.

## Scheduled out of the register

| # | Finding | Scheduled | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| C332 | **Onboarding needs the lab and NetBox to finish, and its first commit leaves out the manifest**: phase two's rotate step's preflight requires the root-owned Oxidized helper (`credential_rotation.py` ~1006-1011), and its NetBox step fails with writes off (the default) or NetBox absent, so the device stays pending; the onboarding commit stages only `host_vars/<device>.yml`, so the new manifest entry is committed only with the first `save_golden` (against 'every commit that moves a device carries the manifest'). Static and DHCP on IOS-XE work only on the emulated image (no SSH key generated, `password 0` form); classic IOS cannot be onboarded by any method (blocked pending measurement), so classic-IOS ZTP has no code; on DHCP a person writes the Kea reservation; the v2 pending page does not show the ZTP stage; the render step's output is discarded (the download re-renders). Page: onboard. Found 2026-10-02 by the agent drafting the manual page named, reading the code; not re-measured. | **[C, functional → NSOT_PLAN P.17, P.18; sorted 2026-10-02]** 2026-10-02: NSOT_PLAN P.17 (classic IOS, with Stage 10's multi-vendor item) and P.18 (phase two and rotation without the lab, before Stage 10's release) | NSOT_PLAN.md P.17, P.18 |
| C290 | **The tool could not change a running IP SLA operation** (the operator, 2026-10-01: s3's probe to r1, every 10 s, to be slowed to 60 s through the tool, C93). IOS refuses to modify a scheduled entry ("Entry already running and cannot be modified (only can delete (no) and start over)"), so an intent edit sent as the ordinary merge (`ip sla 1` / ` frequency 60`) would be refused by the device and rolled back, and a restore to a ref with another frequency the same. **Built 2026-10-01** (`modules/nsot/recreate.py`, wired into `routes/deploy._program`, now the one program computation for the deploy plan and apply, the pipeline's run, and the restore's preview and apply, which had each called `merge_commands` themselves): an operation the device runs and intent defines differently is deleted, re-created from intent and rescheduled, previewed with the definition it replaces, verified by reading it back, rolled back by restoring the old definition from the snapshot, and kept in the rolled-back block. | C189 | **The app log records an ERROR, "The WebSocket transport is not available", that began today** (found reading the host log for C188, 2026-09-28). engine.io logs it when a client asks to upgrade to a WebSocket and the server's async mode cannot serve one, so the client stays on long-polling. The contract still holds over long-polling, as recorded. The fix is the serving stack: the production server keeps the threading mode, `simple-websocket` joins the lock, and nginx proxies the upgrade. **Measured again 2026-10-03 (via LAN, read-only, the app log since 05:31Z), after the operator saw `data-live="unknown"` in the served page and 14,010 `/socket.io/` requests with one 101:** 105 handshakes, all 200; 105 distinct sessions; 4,975 long-polling exchanges on them, all 200; no WebSocket request in the app's log. The served page's `unknown` is the value before any script runs (`base.html`); the script redraws it every second from the socket's state, so only the live DOM can say connected (`document.getElementById('nmas-live').dataset.live`).
