| C178 | **Verify passes a BGP break that has not yet reset the session: it returns at its FIRST passing read, 10 s in, while IOS holds a session to its 180 s hold time** (found reading `convergence.wait_for` for C117's unknown (1), 2026-09-28). `wait_for` returns `converged` at the first read satisfying its check, and BGP's first read comes at `initial_wait` (10 s); the window's 60 s is the most it WAITS for a recovery, never how long it WATCHES. A deploy that breaks BGP without resetting the TCP session at once (a filter on TCP 179, a lost route to a multihop peer, perhaps the update-source going down: C117's (1)) reads Established at 10 s, passes, rolls back nothing, and the receipt records the checks as passed. It is the verify's claim outrunning what it looked at: "verified" means "up 10 s after the push". The tenth verify defect (C62, C64 to C68, C108, C114, C115), and the operator will measure it on the throwaway before `timers` is added, so it is confirmed on a real device rather than inferred from the code. C117's acceptance sidesteps it with `timers 1 3`; the fleet's own BGP runs default timers (r3, r4). | **[C, F → 7.3; sorted 2026-09-28]** **BUILT 2026-09-29 overnight, awaiting two real steps.** Verify now reads BGP once more no earlier than the hold time after the push (`pipeline._watch_bgp_hold`): a session gone by then FAILS verify and triggers the rollback; one unreadable fails too, never passes; the push time is stamped per device; the receipt and the result say how long each device was watched, against which hold time and why (`tests/test_bgp_hold_watch.py`, from r3's real config and its real `show bgp all summary`, the break a minimal edit of it; three controls fired). The hold time is the CONFIGURED one (`neighbor timers`, then peer-group, then `timers bgp`, then IOS's 180 s), an upper bound on the negotiated one, so the wait is conservative. **Cost, stated**: a deploy or restore to a BGP device (r3, r4) now takes up to 180 s longer. (1) **Done 2026-09-29**: r3's `show bgp all neighbors` shows hold time 180 and keepalive 60 on both sessions, so the 180 s wait is the right bound there. And one fact on the live fleet: the IPv6 peer reads "Route to peer address reachability Up: 1; Down: 1" with "Connections established 1; dropped 0", so the route to the peer went down once and the SESSION NEVER DROPPED. That is IOS holding a session through a lost route, supporting C178's premise, not proof, since how long the route was down is not known. Awaiting (2) C117's real-device loop (does a filter on TCP 179 hold the session to hold expiry). Originally: build: a BGP check that does not pass before the negotiated hold time has elapsed since the push (read per peer from `show bgp all neighbors`), and a receipt that says for how long each session was watched; measure first on the throwaway whether a filter on TCP 179 holds the session to hold expiry (the case that needs no IOS unknown) | `modules/nsot/convergence.py` `wait_for`; `modules/pipeline.py` verify |

The durable direction, the heartbeat rules' precedent: rules as code, generated and tested against real captured lines, so a rule that cannot match what the fleet produces fails a test instead of reading as quiet. **SURVEYED 2026-09-28 (probes 5 and 6, run on the host, read-only through Grafana's proxy; 7 days of each rule's own query, 30 days of state history).** Seven rules:<br>- `Device unreachable (SNMP)`, `up < 1` for 2 m: CAN fire (`up` reached 0 in 7 days); 54 Normal and 46 Pending, never Alerting, so every dip was shorter than 2 minutes;<br>- `Interface down`, `ifOperStatus > 1 and ifAdminStatus == 1`: its inputs exist (73 series each), the condition never held in 7 days, and it has alerted 6 times in 30. **Not blind: quiet**, and its no-data setting is `NoData`, so a healthy network reads as no-data (C166's second defect, here alone);<br>- `gRPC telemetry stream lost`, `count by (source) (...) < 1`: **MISCODED, and the evidence is in its own data.** A lost source's series vanishes rather than reading 0, so the count can never be below 1; the source count fell from 4 to 2 in the last 7 days, and the rule recorded 5 `Normal (MissingSeries)` and never Alerting. It resolves exactly when it should fire;<br>- `Critical syslog received`: miscoded by naming (C166);<br>- `Interface error rate elevated`, rate > 0: inputs present (73 series), 0 for 7 days: quiet, and can fire;<br>- `Interface output discards`: fires (10 Alerting in 30 days);<br>- `IP SLA probe failing`, `!= 1` for 2 m: **WORKS** (measured at 1-minute resolution, 2026-09-28): its failures are one-minute timeouts (sense 4), 73 runs in 7 days across six probes, and the one run of 4 minutes (s3, 09-22 23:43) did go Pending, then Alerting, then Normal. "49 Pending, never Alerting" was read from a history capped at 100 rows (the window standing in for all time, mine); for the same reason "Device unreachable never alerted" is unproven.<br>**Every history verdict re-read WITHOUT A CAP (2026-09-28, the operator's sweep):** one day per request, and a window returning a full page split and re-read (the time unit is microseconds). The query-window verdicts (can fire, quiet this week, miscoded) stand as stated. **Two real incidents the old rules missed**, measured in the gRPC rule's own data: 09-22 23:41, about 20 minutes with NO telemetry source reporting (the same minutes as IP SLA's one long failure, so one fleet-level event seen by two rules), and 09-28 08:51 to 09:01, r1 and r3 absent. `count by (source) (...) < 1` recorded neither; the replacement fires on both (replayed before it was written).<br>**And the syslog stream's `host` label is the COLLECTOR** (`nmas` on all 3,151 lines in 24 h), so a syslog rule's instance carries no device: the heartbeat rules get it by matching the line (`\s<host>:\s`), and C166's rule cannot say which device. For 8.6 that is an instance with no member. **And the SNMP TARGETS are a hand-kept list too** (measured 2026-09-28 by the Grafana reader's address resolution, read-only on the host): the rules' polled `instance` addresses are nine, eight resolve to inventory devices, and `10.255.1.15` (r5, RETIRED) is still polled by two rules, so its alerts would read "matches no device's address in the inventory" (correctly); and **r6 is polled by none**, monitored by its heartbeat alone. | C92 | **The online dot is one probe, five seconds old at best, and a single miss draws "offline"** (the operator, 2026-09-27). `connection.ping_worker` probes every device of the ACTIVE list every 5 s (`is_device_online`: ICMP with a 2 s timeout, then TCP:22 with a 2 s timeout) and overwrites an in-memory `device_status_cache[ip] = bool`: no count, no time, no history. **Measured from the host's app log**, which records every transition at INFO (7,061 transitions, 2026-09-23 07:52 to 09-27 17:59, 106 hours): 2,946 offline runs, and **2,751 of them (93%) ended at the very next probe**. s3 alone accounts for 1,540 runs (14.5 an hour, one every four minutes), and 1,362 of its 1,478 single-probe misses had no other device down at the same time. Every other device's traffic to the NMAS crosses s3's `Vlan99`, so s3 was forwarding while its own control plane did not answer in 2 s. The two switches with the slowest measured clocks (C9) miss most: s3 (0.55 to 0.61) 1,540 and s4 (0.75) 227, against 161 to 192 for every other device (r5, retired partway, 91). That is a correlation (a starved vIOS answers slowly), not a proven cause. **A threshold measured from those runs:** at two consecutive misses the fleet's offline runs fall from 2,946 to 195, and at three to 99; the log's whole-second times make that approximate, and a reader counting misses makes it exact. Genuine outages look different: runs over 60 s mostly come 6 to 8 devices at once (2026-09-26 00:12, 08:33 and 08:41; 2026-09-27 08:42), which is the path to the fleet rather than a device. One run (s3, 92 minutes, 09-27 02:34) is not attributed; the transition log cannot show whether the app itself stopped inside it. s3's own pattern is C93. **Four defects in one value:**<br>- one miss is "not answering right now", drawn as "offline";<br>- "online" is three claims, and the dot makes one without saying which: answered a probe from the NMAS within 2 s (this), the device's own heartbeat arrived in its window (P.1), and the tool can log in (SSH, phase 2's definition of reached);<br>- the dot has no age, so a stale value and a fresh one look the same, and `.get(ip, False)` draws a device never probed (another list, a NetBox-sourced list with no CSV, the first cycle) as offline (§14's finding, still true of the dot);<br>- it is not only a dot: `refresh_hostnames`, Auto-Create (a skip reason "offline"), the topology link-status reads (`/topology/link_status`, `/topology/proto_link_status`), the AI device context and the agent's bulk targeting all act on one probe.<br>**Cost at scale** (§0a): 900 devices every 5 s is 180 probes a second from one host, and a failed probe costs up to 4 s of a 20-thread pool, so a partial outage stretches the cycle it is measuring. **The case that sets the priority** (C70, the operator, 2026-09-27): r2's dot went offline immediately after the scoped restore changed r2's GigabitEthernet2, its core link, on the path in-band management uses (the change itself was IPv6-only). For about thirty seconds the operator believed the restore had cut in-band management. r2 was fine: ping 5 of 5, 23 to 31 ms. So the problem is not that the dot is noisy. It is noisy in a way indistinguishable from the failure people most fear, at the moment they are most primed to believe it: right after a change to the path the tool reaches the device on ("the repair path travels over the thing being changed"). **And on the deployment host the dot does not ping at all** (measured the same day): `ping3` raises `PermissionError` as the service user (`flask-app` runs as `<user>`, no capabilities, `net.ipv4.ping_group_range` is `1 0`). `is_device_online()` catches it, logs it at DEBUG, and falls through to TCP:22 with a 2 s timeout. | C106 | **CLIs that stop half-way, leaving a state nobody is told about** (the operator's question after `nmas-deploy` moved the checkout and could not restart, 2026-09-27; surveyed by reading every script, 1a VERIFIED and FIXED, the rest from the code read and not yet verified). (1a) `rotate()` set "rotated and committed" whatever `_commit` answered and then DELETED the staging copy, so a failed credential-store write left a device holding a password recorded nowhere, reported as "live and recorded": fixed (state `ROTATED_NOT_RECORDED`, the copy kept and named, danger first, a job-health row). Recovery is by hand from the kept copy: a `--record-staged` path does not exist yet. (1b) the persist chain's `oxidized_row` ran `sudo -n` with no pre-check (the operator, next after C70: an unchecked sudo in the rotation path is B15's shape). Verified: a refused `sudo -n` printed nothing on stdout, the stage parsed `{}`, and the chain failed with NO reason after the device was already rotated and committed. **FIXED 2026-09-27**: preflight asks `sudo -n -l <helper>` before anything changes the device and refuses naming the exact sudoers line; the stage checks sudo's exit code and names a refusal; an answer without `ok` is not success. Measured on the host: the narrow rule exists and the service runs as the user it covers, so nothing is refused there today. (The danger sentence printing last remains.) (2) `oxidized-to-config.sh` can overwrite some labs' startup configs and then fail a later copy, the read-back or the commit; a failed backup is ignored (`cp -r … 2>/dev/null`), and a failed commit still ends "Startup-configs updated". (3) `nmas-retire` clears the credential before a step that can fail, and its refusal omits what was already done (it is resumable). (4) `nmas-netbox-backup` pruned old backups before the new one had shipped, and by AGE alone: while shipping failed, the local copies were the only copies and were deleted as they aged, so a failed run left fewer backups than before (the operator: fix first). **FIXED 2026-09-27**: ship first, prune last, and an expired backup is deleted only once recorded as shipped to every destination its tier needs (`shipped.json`); an unshipped one is kept, named in the run and first in `--status`, and retried each run. **Deletion is keyed on the PROPERTY, not a proxy for it** (the operator): age was the proxy, existence elsewhere is the property, the register's recurring lesson applied to retention. And the retry converts a permanent gap into a delay: a missed daily used to be lost for good and now ships late, so the off-box copy's completeness no longer depends on nothing having failed on any given night. (5) `nmas-bulk-intent` and three `nsot_*` repair scripts leave written, uncommitted files, reported as "REFUSED", which reads as nothing having happened (the standing rule inverted: the message says nothing happened, the filesystem disagrees, and the person acts on the message). **Bulk intent FIXED 2026-09-27**: a failed commit puts every written file back as committed and says "nothing changed", or leads with LEFT UNCOMMITTED naming the paths; the three repair scripts remain. Also: `nmas-deploy` exits with a traceback and no audit row if `git fetch` or `merge` fails; `nmas-persist-native` exits 1 ("not persisted") if recording raises after the device saved. **The pattern (the operator's):** a tool that does part of an operation and stops, and tells the person running it but not the exit code or anything that watches. `nmas-deploy` is fixed (it refuses before moving when it cannot restart, exit 6; a failed restart leads with MIXED VERSION; a `running-version` job-health row). **The ordering, checked against each remaining item** (the operator's question after 1b, 2026-09-27: does the irreversible part come first, with the blocker found second?). Read from the code: **(2) the containerlab sync: YES, three times.** A lab's startup configs are overwritten, and a LATER lab's copy failure is found afterwards. The loop continues and the count check refuses with exit 1, while the first lab stays overwritten and uncommitted. The next run's reconcile can record it late. The per-lab backup runs as `cp -r … 2>/dev/null; rsync …`. The `;` means a failed backup does not stop the overwrite it exists to protect. Whether a destination can commit (repo present, git identity) is found only after every file is written. The run then ends with "Startup-configs updated." as its LAST line, after the failure block. The fix has three parts. Check every destination's preconditions before copying any. Gate each rsync on its backup (`&&`). Lead with the state. **(3) nmas-retire: YES.** It clears the device's credential override FIRST (irreversible: the value is gone), then writes the declaration, then commits. The commit can fail (`manifest.release`, git), and on failure the device is still in the inventory, managed, with its override gone. The CLI then leads with "REFUSED:", which reads as nothing having happened. The fix is to commit first (the commit already puts the tree back on failure), delete both credentials last, and have a stop lead with what was done. **(5) the three repair scripts: NO, the same symptom by a different mechanism.** Nothing irreversible happens before the commit. They write working-tree files, and a failed commit leaves them written. The fix is bulk intent's: put the files back as committed. **`nmas-persist-native`: NO.** The device save is the operation, and a failed record after it is a false REPORT ("not persisted"), not a late-found blocker. **`nmas-deploy`'s fetch/merge: NO.** A failed `merge --ff-only` leaves the checkout unmoved, so only the traceback is wrong. **1b's danger sentence printing last** is the report half of the same shape, and stays open. **(2) the containerlab sync, FIXED 2026-09-27 (the operator's order: the `;` first):** a lab is copied only after its OWN backup succeeded (the `;` let a failed backup go on to overwrite the configs it protected). A failure is named for its step: file list, BACKUP FAILED, or COPY FAILED after the backup (restore from it). Attempts are counted apart from successes, so a failed lab is no longer blamed on the loop ("ran fewer times") or the copy ("reported success ... do not match"), both false. Only the copied labs are read back and committed. The staged files are kept while any lab failed, and the summary leads with "NOT ALL LABS UPDATED: N of M" and exits 3. Tested by executing the shipped region under bash against real directories, with one lab's backup refused; a control that stops the failed backup from stopping its copy fails both tests. **The second half, FIXED the same day (the operator: fix it with the rest; the third script with this shape):** before any copy, each destination is asked whether it can take a commit (its git directory writable, no `index.lock` left behind; the identity rides on every commit already). A lab that cannot is refused BEFORE its copy, named "CANNOT COMMIT (reason); NOTHING was copied". A lab with no repository is still copied and reported NOT VERSIONED, a declared state named every run. Tested with a repository holding a stale `index.lock`; control: the skip removed fails it. | **[C, F → 7.3 for (3): retire clears the credential before a commit that can fail, so a failed retire leaves a device MANAGED WITH NO CREDENTIAL (A if it fires); 7.3 builds retire as an operation, commit first, and (3) is its acceptance. The rest, M: (1b)'s danger sentence printing last, (5)'s three repair scripts, `nmas-persist-native`'s false report, `nmas-deploy`'s traceback. (2) is FIXED (bfde4ef, d7efb9d). Re-sorted 2026-09-28 (it was UNKNOWN, sorted from a truncated read; see the Re-sort section)]** build: each, in the order above; verify each before fixing (a code read is a claim); the rule for all: check what the later step needs before the earlier step, and a stop's FIRST line names the state and the one command | **7.3** (moved from the open sections 2026-09-28: a stage owns it). this file, 2026-09-27 |

Held to 180 s: exactly C178's case. **The original three:** (1) whether IOS drops the session when its update-source goes down or holds it to the 180 s hold timer. A single-series alert on a rule whose query A is PromQL ending in a threshold is now a `grafana/series` row, keyed on its series, so a re-fire is the same row. **Acknowledge…** on it measures the series' 7-day p95 from the rule's own query without its threshold and records it as the band. (c) The refusal told a person to "pass acknowledge_structural_change=True", which no screen can do. (d) **Found reading the host log:** the guard had refused each vty deploy's own golden commit too (r1, r2, r6, r3, r4 at 23:19 to 23:25, logged at ERROR), and the deploy result drew a clean success whose record said "nothing succeeded, or no capture changed". | 2026-10-01 | **FIXED (this commit).** (a) A shrink is accepted when the capture IS what committed intent renders (`intent_match` state `match`), recorded on the commit as `Structural-Change: r1 line 5->3, which is what committed intent renders`; an unexplained shrink is still refused. (b) `save_golden()` judges each device alone: a refused device is left out of the commit with its own reason (`refused`), counted as skipped so no baseline is taken, and the others are recorded; the working tree is never left dirty. The capture names each device's own outcome (`not_recorded`) and leads with it. (c) The capture preview draws the shrink as a gate: passed when intent explains it, otherwise failing with a reason field beside it; the reason (the authorisation's shape rule) is recorded on the commit as the person's. (d) A deploy whose golden was refused or not recorded is `partial`, names each device ("the change is on the device and its golden was NOT recorded") and says to capture it. Measured read-only on the host first: r3's config (as Oxidized holds it) loses line 5->3 and matches intent, so it is explained; the four switches match intent with nothing lost. Five controls. |

| C281 | **Every read on a tool session was bounded by Netmiko's fixed 10 s and 20 s, below what a slow device takes, and the hourly startup check drew seven slow reads as Critical** (the operator, 2026-10-01, reported as a C272 regression: at 06:03, 7 of 9 UNKNOWN, exit 2, the unit failing). **Not C272:** the startup check calls `send_command` directly and never reaches `run_device_command`; no connection setting changed (`fast_cli` since 09-21, `conn_timeout` since C205, no `read_timeout_override`); and the journal holds the same timeouts at 00:06 (2), 03:02 (3) and 04:02 (1), before C272 was deployed. C272's part was to stop hiding them in the app: before it, `run_device_command`'s re-send absorbed the same echo timeouts ("prompt-based read failed", dozens a day in the log). **The cause:** Netmiko 4.3 waits a FIXED 10 s for a command's echo (`command_echo_read`, whatever `read_timeout` the caller gives), 10 s for the login prompt and for `terminal width 511`, 20 s for `terminal length 0`, and the 06:03 rows failed at exactly those four (r1 to r4 the echo, s1 the prompt, s3 and s4 session preparation). Measured on the host the same morning, every device at once and then one at a time: an echo up to 5.9 s, a login up to 46 s, a vIOS `show running-config \| include ^username` 31.6 s alone (s3); at 06:03 the reachability reader also lost s1 and r4 for 15 to 30 s. **The echo is not the defect, and the measurement showed why:** a probe that ended each read on the prompt alone was answered by the prompts logging in left in the channel (`\nr1#\nr1#\nr1#\nr1#show startup-config`) and returned every command the previous one's output. | 2026-10-01 | **FIXED (this commit).** `open_ssh()` opens the session with `auto_connect=False`, applies `floor_reads()`, then logs in: every read on a tool session (login, session preparation, each echo) waits at least `read_floor()`, the config read's measured bound (120 s), and a caller's longer bound is kept. A show command ends on `prompt_terminator()`, the device's own prompt alone on the last line AFTER the echo, so a hostname inside the output cannot end it and Netmiko's per-command prompt probe (five extra newlines, each with its own fixed bound) is gone. The startup check: an unreadable device is ONE job-health row naming the devices, with since when (each device's state now carries `since` across runs) and what to do (it retries at the next run; a warning, with the command, after three runs); a startup config that lacks the credential keeps its own danger row; the script exits 2 only when it read no device. Tests: `tests/test_session_reads_wait_for_the_device.py`, REAL r1 (IOS-XE) and s1 (vIOS) channel transcripts (`tests/fixtures/transcripts/`) replayed through real Netmiko 4.3 and 4.6 on a simulated clock (the 06:03 echo timeout reproduced with the host's exact message, leftover prompts, a hostname mid-configuration, a 25 s login); `tests/test_c53_device_stage.py` (the 06:03 rows). Controls: the floor removed fails the four delay tests; the terminator removed fails the fourteen transcripts (the prompt probe's extra writes). |
| C280 | **The publication gate counted OCCURRENCES, not secrets** (the operator, 2026-10-01, on C278). It held four pushes because the published `snmp_community` count rose from 9 to 10, and r6 had received the SAME community the other eight devices carry, a value already acknowledged as published. One more copy changes no exposure, yet under that rule every onboarding and every monitoring-profile apply would freeze pushes until someone re-acknowledged, which trains people to acknowledge without reading. | 2026-10-01 | **FIXED (this commit).** The gate is keyed on secret VALUES: each live secret has a fingerprint (HMAC-SHA256 under a per-list salt kept in `remote.json`, 12 hex, never the value and not checkable against a guess), the acknowledgement records each fingerprint with the devices it appears in, and a push is held only for a new KIND or a fingerprint not acknowledged, red at once with the kind, the fingerprint and where (C278). Another copy of an acknowledged value is reported (`copies`), never held. The Remote card and the acknowledge preview list each secret by kind, fingerprint and devices. An acknowledgement recorded before fingerprints (the host's, 05:08) keeps the count rule and says to acknowledge once more. Found building it: `acknowledge()` saved the copy of `remote.json` it read before the scan, which would have dropped a salt the scan created and changed every fingerprint; it re-reads after the scan, tested. The scan finds the salt from the repository's own path, never by resolving the list's name, which creates its directory (C51). SNMPv3 (Stage 9, C249) is the real fix: it removes the community from the configurations altogether, recorded there. Control: the count rule put back fails the four value-rule tests. |
| C278 | **Four commits sat on the host for four hours, each HELD at the publication gate, and the screens said only "not pushed"** (the operator, 2026-10-01: "4 commit(s) on Default not pushed ... (oldest: 9b1ea13, 3 h 55 min)"). Measured in the host's log: 9b1ea13 (01:11), e863245 (01:14), 2fbcf5d (03:41) and 0e83e02 (05:01) each logged `archive: auto-push HELD for 'Default': what would be published has grown since it was acknowledged: more snmp_community` (r6's golden had gained its community, so the acknowledged count, 9, was exceeded). An unpushed commit with no hold turns red past `UNPUSHED_DANGER_S` (five reads, 10 min; a commit pushes in seconds). A tag a hook call NAMED and could not send (held, or a failed push) is kept (`pending_tags`) and sent by the next push, still named one by one: the explicit-refspec rule holds, and a first version that computed the tags the remote lacked was withdrawn before commit, because it would republish a withdrawn baseline deleted on the remote (two existing tests refused it). The reader measures the remote: the tool's tags it lacks on HEAD's history, withdrawn baselines left out (`tags_not_pushed`). The structural rule, extended: every exit of the push hook that does not publish is recorded where the reader reads it (AST, with a planted control). Two controls, each failing only its own tests. |

| C272 | **Every command re-sent on a session still carrying the first one's output, and verify passed or failed on what it could not read** (found with C271, 2026-10-01; the operator: "a verify that can't read reliably must say so, not pass or fail on stitched text"). `run_device_command` probed a `show` for 10 s, then re-sent it with `send_command_timing` on the SAME session, and verify's own reads (`show ip ospf`, `show ip bgp`, `show interfaces`) were in that short-probe list, read right after a push and a save, when the deploy's session read its prompt as `^@`. Each routing read's failure was `except: pass`, so a protocol whose read could not be trusted read before the push as "not running" (never checked) and after it as a loss, which rolled back. | 2026-10-01 | **FIXED (this commit).** Every command is sent ONCE, waited for with the config read's measured bound (120 s), never re-sent: the probe and its slow-command list are gone. A session whose prompt reads as NUL bytes is asked for it again (`set_base_prompt`) before anything is sent, and refused if it still does. A show reply holding an echoed command or the session's own prompt raises `UnreliableRead`. A read that raises SPENDS its session (`config_read.SPENT_ATTR`): nothing reads it again, and the connection pool replaces it, so a later read gets a new session. The pipeline's reads ask the pool at each read (`_conn_of`), so one bad read leaves the rest of a snapshot readable. Before the push, a snapshot read that cannot be trusted refuses the deploy with nothing sent, naming the command and why. After it, the post snapshot and the canary read on a NEW session (the push's is closed), a routing read that failed goes to the settle window to be read again on a new session, and what still cannot be trusted (a protocol, routes, interfaces, the config the removals are checked against, BGP at its hold time) is `unreadable`: verify does not pass, does not fail on it, rolls nothing back for it, and the receipt, the result ("could not be read reliably after the change ... capture the device to read it again") and Needs attention say so. Five controls, each failing only the tests aimed at it (tests/test_verify_reads_reliably.py, on r3's real captures through the real `run_device_command`). |

Tested on a frozen clock: two in one second are two files and two entries, eight threads at once lose nothing, a delete keeps the other; the old overwrite put back fails three. |

The two earlier retries are a DIFFERENT mechanism: each came 60 s after a deploy's push, on the deploy's own session, with Netmiko's prompt read as `^@` (NUL bytes after the push), so the first read consumed the whole output while waiting and the retry read a clean channel (the golden scan agrees: none stitched). | 2026-10-01 | **FIXED (this commit).** `modules/config_read.py`: ONE `show running-config`, waited for with the configured bound (`nsot_config_read_timeout`, 120 s), never retried on its session, and judged before it is returned or recorded. A refusal reads "the device's output could not be read reliably", names every sign, and records nothing. It applies at the capture (Save All and per device: the read and the preview's "device read" gate), in `run_device_command` for any configuration read whoever calls it (drift, the pipeline's snapshot, topology, the agent), in drift (which also closes the pooled session), and in `save_golden`, which every recording path passes (deploy, restore, rotation, adopt, onboarding): there on evidence only, since the reader judged presence. The paths that call `send_command` directly never retry, so they cannot stitch. Controls: the retry put back fails its three tests, and the save check removed fails its own. |

and `_commit_paths` (`save_templates`, `save_host_vars`: templates, intent edits, seed, revert, bulk intent; retire directly); BYPASSING it: abandon, the rename commit (`apply_pending_renames`, the Sync device names path), both migration commits, and `config_git`'s first commit; `tests/test_every_commit_publishes.py` fails on any git call naming `commit` outside it (an AST scan of the program and its scripts, with a floor, subprocess lists included), on a deferred commit its caller never publishes, and on `run_post_commit` reached around `publish()`; controls: abandon put back fails the scan and the real-abandon test, `_commit_paths` without `publish()` fails the deferral rule. **Visible:** the `remote-publication` reader compares HEAD with the remote's own branch (`git ls-remote` through the repository's own `origin`, 10 s bound, measured 0.99 s on the host), every 120 s and after every commit (a post-commit hook after the push); its one sentence (`remote_publication.describe()`) is drawn by the Git tab ("Everything is committed · 1 commit(s) not pushed to <remote> (oldest: <sha>, 12 min)", or "and pushed"), the Remote card, and a Needs attention row with the push as its action. An unreadable `remote.json` still gets an answer through `origin`, and says C172. Measured on the host before the fix, read-only: HEAD `7a72258`, GitHub `main` `cf4d96d`. **Accepted on the host, 2026-09-29, after `0dd102b`'s deploy:** Needs attention showed "1 commit(s) on Default not pushed … (oldest: 7a72258, 52 min)", compared 7 s earlier; the operator pressed Push now at 21:52:21Z and GitHub's `main` read `7a72258` (read-only `ls-remote` afterwards). Two presentation defects from that run, fixed the same turn: the row's action named "the Git tab's Remote card", and the card is on the DEVICES tab (the operator had to ask where the button was); and a manual push goes around the post-commit hooks, so the row would have stood up to a reader cycle after the push (the push route re-reads now). |

So, by what is lost: **(1)** **the laptop alone**: no NetBox data is lost. Up to a day is lost, and the Proxmox copies are unreadable; **(3)** **the laptop and the Proxmox host**: the B2 dailies are the only copies left, and **nothing can read them**. This is the scenario the off-host copy exists for, and it fails in it. **The same laptop also holds the break-glass record and, in its password manager, that record's passphrase**, so it is also the only OFF-HOST copy of `data/key.key` (B5's escrow). In case (3) the key goes with it: the VM and its image are on the lost host. One laptop is the single off-host holder of both keys. **Closes when** the gpg secret key, and the break-glass record with it, exist somewhere that does not fail with this laptop: a USB copy kept elsewhere, or a password-manager entry that syncs off it. The unproven 849-byte export was removed from Proxmox, because a copy nobody has proven is the one somebody reaches for. **The route that failed first is the finding:** the OpenPGP export had a matching sha256 on both sides, the right key id and the right modes, and could not be imported. Every check short of the decrypt passed (CLAUDE.md, *a check that the artefact ARRIVED cannot see whether it WORKS there*). **The placement departs from NETBOX_BACKUP section 1's heading ("NOT Proxmox")**, which gave no reason; the reasoning and what it does not cover (losing the laptop and Proxmox together leaves B2 unreadable) are in NETBOX_BACKUP's custody note. **The passphrase is now the remaining single point of failure**, the operator's to keep off-machine. Carried forward: C144 (the restore test never asserted the encrypted copies) and C145 (the import failure, and the paperkey unproven). The history of the row: **NOT CLOSED: the first copy FAILED its proof (the operator's run, 2026-09-28), and the proof is what caught it.** The export was copied to Proxmox with matching hashes, and the check that decrypts a real B2 backup from an EMPTY keyring refused: `error sending to agent: Bad secret key` on the cv25519 subkey's import, then `No SmartCard daemon` at the decrypt. The matching hashes proved only that the same bytes were copied. **Measured afterwards, read-only:** (1) the laptop holds the REAL key: `gpg -K` shows `sec`/`ssb` with no `#` or `>`, both keygrips are agent type D (disk) with protection P, and no smartcard is configured. (2) The 849-byte export is NOT a stub (the operator's reading, corrected): parsed in an empty keyring, both packets carry iter+salt S2K-protected secret material, never `gnu-dummy` or divert-to-card, and 849 bytes is normal for ed25519 plus cv25519. So the failure is in the IMPORT, not the export: a mistyped passphrase or GnuPG's own conversion of this subkey, not yet told apart. (3) **The restore test decrypts nothing**: `nmas-netbox-restore-test` restores the newest LOCAL hourly backup, which is plain. It proves the dump restores and says nothing about the encrypted copies. The one proof those can be read is NETBOX_BACKUP §6f, 2026-09-26, the laptop's own keyring decrypting a real B2 copy. (4) `~/nmas-backup-paperkey.txt` restores through the SAME import, so it is not a proven second copy until it passes the same empty-keyring decrypt. **Next:** decrypt a real B2 backup with the laptop's keyring (can anyone read them today), then copy the agent's own key files, which are already passphrase-protected and need no export or import, and prove the copy by the same decrypt in a keyring built only from what Proxmox holds. Earlier the same night: **TRIAGE: data loss, fixed tonight, never Stage 9 (2026-09-28).** The UNKNOWN is measured: the operator answered that NO second copy exists, so a laptop loss makes every Proxmox and B2 copy unreadable. With B9's Object Lock the off-box copies can no longer be deleted, which makes them safe AND unreadable. Measured on the laptop: key `38AB129536505C0A7BE4995D5434D4489191526F`, both subkeys passphrase-protected (gpg-agent `P`), and `~/nmas-backup-paperkey.txt` names the same key. The laptop's `b2:` remote downloads a real backup at its listed size (366,283 bytes). **The fix is a copy, not a build:** the exported secret key (still passphrase-protected) goes to the Proxmox host (<hypervisor>, root by key, gpg present), a failure domain the laptop does not share. A root there already holds the VM disks and their plain local backups, so the copy costs no confidentiality. It is PROVEN only by importing the copy, fetched back from Proxmox, into an EMPTY keyring and decrypting a real B2 backup with it: a copy of the wrong key looks exactly like a working one, and the decrypt also proves the passphrase is known. The passphrase must itself survive the laptop. docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §1 |

| C42 | **A test passed in CI and failed on the host, and the lock cannot pin the difference: the KERNEL** (found by the operator's `--offline` control run, 2026-09-26). `test_the_session_guard_sees_a_change` failed on the host at `1319890`, a commit CI had passed. Measured with `scripts/nmas-env-facts`, the same probe in both places: the host (kernel 6.8, ext4, CONFIG_HZ=1000) stamps times from a **1 ms tick**, and a directory's mtime did NOT move after a create in **1,837 of 2,000** tries; the laptop (kernel 7.0, tmpfs) moves it every time. The control made a directory and wrote into it within microseconds. The GUARD is sound: it compares the store as it was before the session with its state after, and no test writes in the same millisecond as a change made before pytest started. The CONTROL was not. | C112 | **A rollback that FAILED is drawn as "rolled back"** (the operator's question (4), "what happens if the rollback itself fails", answered from the code, 2026-09-27). `_stage_rollback()` catches a failure per device into `ctx.rollback_failures` and carries on, then sets `rollback_performed = True` and `final_status = "rolled_back"` whatever happened. It is the most dangerous moment to draw a wrong thing as a working one: the push has already failed and the operator is deciding what to do next. | C120 | **A negative control's restore left stale bytecode, and the next run executed the MUTATED code** (found fixing C114, 2026-09-27). The control changed `_NEIGHBOR_DROP_TOLERANCE = 0` to `1` (same size) and a `cp` restored it within the same second. Python validates a cached `.pyc` by the source's mtime (1 s resolution) and size, so the restored file matched the mutated bytecode, and the run after the restore had tolerance 1 while the file said 0. The following control then failed a test it did not target, which is what exposed it. C42's shape, a control whose timing is tighter than the clock, in the controls themselves. Controls earlier in this session were checked: every other mutation changed the file's size.  **Which past controls could it have touched? Reproduced in a scratch directory, 2026-09-27, and bounded rather than counted** (the operator's question 1: about 330 one-time controls across weeks, and a size-preserving edit such as 0->1, true->false or a swapped operator is the natural shape of a control): **The control's own verdict is sound.** The mutated run executes the mutation, because the mutation moved the file's mtime into a new second, so its cached bytecode no longer matches. **The stale bytecode lands in the NEXT run after the restore,** and only when the restore falls in the same second as the mutation. That means mutate, run and restore all inside one second: a short targeted run, never a full suite (about 2 min). **Its consequence is a wrong post-restore run.** It is visible when that run exercises the mutation (a test fails, which is how C120 was found). It is silent when it does not: a laptop "N passed" after a control can have been partly about mutated code. **No committed code was tested that way.** Every commit's CI runs a fresh checkout with no bytecode, and every commit carrying code passed CI (232d001 aside, C119). So the claims resting on CI hold. **The other failure direction is loud, not silent.** A mutation made in the same second as a previous write of the ORIGINAL file could run the original, but that shows up as a control that did not fire, which was always investigated. **Which past instances were affected is unknowable:** the timing was never recorded. What is knowable is the bound above: control verdicts valid, CI verdicts valid, and some laptop post-restore counts possibly not. From now on, caching is off in the runner, so the question cannot arise again. | 2026-09-28 | **Moved 2026-09-28 by the triage, on the row's own evidence:** **FIXED:** `scripts/nmas-test` exports `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`, so no test run writes bytecode and a mutation can never be cached (full suite 114 s against 110-115 s before: no measurable cost; 0 `.pyc` written). No baseline's other secret positions differ from HEAD: no community, key-string or line password has ever been rotated (C79). **A baseline is a record of a moment, and moments contain credentials: any re-apply is a time machine for secrets as well as for configuration.** Nothing here is deleted. B11's population was defined by the method (GETs), where the property is "returns stored config" (B16's lesson). **The sweep is 7.1's next step, before the onboarding retrofit.** Every store is planted, then every `not_device` POST that computes rather than writes is driven, and its response searched, as `test_no_get_returns_a_stored_secret.py` does for GETs. Its population is the gate table's `not_device` POSTs (34), read from `route_gates.GATES` and `url_map`, and each must declare a body and the status it answers with, so a new computing POST is swept by being declared. Every store is planted by B11's own planting, now shared (`planted_stores`) and extended with the legacy `golden_configs/` store, which neither sweep had planted. The sweep adds what a POST reads that a GET does not: a device's running config as a read returns it now, a second backup, configs a caller supplies, a FakeNetBox, and a list that has what a real list has (seeded templates, r1's intent extracted and committed through the routes, the template approved, a template secret that differs from the device, a second golden). A refusal proves nothing, so the eight routes whose response draws stored config must show the masked slot (either mask). Each route is driven anonymously and as a person. **Its first real run found three leaks, from two stores, fixed here:** `POST /compare_backups` returned both backups' secrets in its diff, and `POST /netbox/safety/import/preview` and `/import_all/preview` returned each device's golden and SNMP community. | C89 | **Capture promotes whatever a device holds to the record of record, and asks nothing** (the operator, 2026-09-27, from the host). A hand change made in the terminal, then Save All, became within four minutes a golden, `baseline/20260927T154517Z` and a commit auto-pushed to GitHub (`last push 15:45:23Z … e63b2e6d`). "Golden" means approved, and the only approval was a button meaning "capture". It survives the terminal lockout, because any unguarded writer (console, RESTCONF, NETCONF, a person on the device) gets the same free promotion the moment anyone captures. **Worse than it looks: capture SILENCES the drift checker.** After it, the golden equals the device, so drift against the golden reads clean. **Measured 2026-09-27** (`/deploy/plan` over the fleet, which reads only and sends nothing): 8 of 9 devices' committed intent matches their capture, and r2's differs by exactly the break (1 added, 1 removed). The plan already offers the repair (`interface GigabitEthernet2` / ` ipv6 ospf 1 area 0` / `exit`). So the signal capture erases (device against golden) is not the one that survives it (capture against INTENT). **My read of the four options:** (a) YES: capture is an approval of content, so confirm what you are enshrining. The preview shows each device's diff against its current golden AND where it departs from committed intent; Save All is the fleet form of the same operation, confirmed per device by capture hash (C82's shape). (c) YES, defined against INTENT, never the golden, since at capture the golden becomes the capture and the comparison is empty by construction. A `baseline/` tag needs coverage AND every device's capture matching its committed intent. Measured, that denies exactly `20260927T154517Z` today and nothing else, so it does not refuse on its first day; E7's golden state then adds "working". (d) YES, but as a computed FACT rather than a claim about who looked: every golden commit, whatever the path (Save All, a deploy's stage 8.5, an approval, onboarding, a CLI), carries `Intent-Match: yes` or `Intent-Match: no: <device> (<n> lines)`, computed at commit time in one place. That is what makes the 3am case visible without anybody deciding to look. (b) NO as a refusal: a golden is the record of the device, and refusing to capture a device's real state makes the record lie. "Captured, but not a baseline, and marked" is (c) plus (d). **And in 7.2:** a device whose golden departs from committed intent is a Needs-attention row, because after a capture it is the only signal left. | 2026-09-27 | **DECIDED by the operator and scheduled into 7.1 step 4.** Stated exactly (the operator): the 3am case is not silent, it is SILENT IN DRIFT, the signal people actually watch. "The record was corrupted" and "one of two comparisons was corrupted" are different problems, and only the second has a cheap fix. Capture corrupts the comparison against the GOLDEN, which it becomes; the comparison against INTENT survives (8 of 9 match, r2 differs by exactly the two lines, and the plan offers the repair). Decided: (a) capture previews each device's diff against its golden and against committed intent and is confirmed per device, with Save All joining; (c) a baseline tag needs every capture to MATCH COMMITTED INTENT, not the golden, which would be empty by construction (the trap of validating a masked render against itself); (d) every golden commit carries a computed `Intent-Match:` trailer, whatever the path, auto-push included, which makes git answer which captures enshrined something nobody intended; (b) refused. **Weighted highest by the operator:** the 7.2 Needs-attention row for a device whose golden departs from its intent, since after a capture it is the only remaining signal and it needs nobody to look deliberately. |

Its first run found C56. **What C55 actually was, measured on the host afterwards:** `collector_config.json` did not exist, so no community had ever been stored, and the values the page served were the code's defaults (`public`, `private`). No fleet golden carries an RW community (onboarding removes vrnetlab's), so there was nothing to rotate. All eight RO lines equal the default `public` by digest, so what went to browsers and the provider was the fleet's real RO community, and a value anyone guesses first. **The sharper half is the design:** the page sent `'public'` on every save with an empty field, so the moment the value stopped arriving, a save would have REPLACED a real community with `public`. A GET returning the value a later save depended on is worse than one leaking a secret; write-only fixed both, and an empty field now saves nothing. |
| C51 | A GET with a list name created that list: `get_list_data_dir()` calls `os.makedirs()`, so asking about a list that does not exist, a typo included, brought `lists/<name>/` into existence. | 2026-09-27 | **Fixed, in Stage 7.0 (the harness step).** Measured first, by a sweep of EVERY GET with an unknown list name in each place a list arrives (`list_name`, `list`, a `list_name` path converter): **24 GETs**, not the one it was found on, and a stack trace per route named each creating call site (route `_repo_for` helpers, `list_goldens`, `sync_targets`, `migrate.plan`, the inventory source, `remote_path`, template seeding). Fixed ONCE at the boundary rather than at 24 sites: `routes/list_param.py` refuses a GET or HEAD naming a list that is neither registered nor on disk, 404, naming it, before any view resolves a path, and says it is not the same as an empty list (an empty answer for a typo is a claim about a list nobody has). `listref.exists()` answers without creating, and `config.list_data_path()` is the one derivation both resolvers use. Writes are left to their routes. `test_reads_create_no_list.py` sweeps every GET for the property (floors: 100 swept, 20 with arguments), with controls both ways: the hook removed brings back all 24, and a real list by name and by slug still reads. Three tests read an unregistered list that existed only as a patched path; they now patch `LISTS_DIR` too, as this file already said tests must. |
| B1 | `skipped_drifted` returned a whole device configuration, secret lines included, in the `/deploy/apply` JSON response. | 2026-09-27 | **Fixed, the gate before Stage 7.0.** `plan_batch()` no longer puts the capture in the skip entry; the two hashes it compared are the diagnostic. The field was carried for a "one-click re-preview" that nothing was ever written to read, and a test pinned it with that reason. Now pinned the other way at both ends: `plan_batch()` carries no config, and the real `/deploy/apply` response for a drifted device holds no line of the capture (the `secret 9` line included). Control: restoring the field fails exactly those two tests. |
| C52 | A ZTP render used the weaker `password 0` form because vrnetlab injects a user line, and nothing injects one on a configless node. | 2026-09-27 | **Fixed.** `secret_clause()` takes `injects_user`, the credential form is a property of the deployment (like the SSH key), and a `ztp` source renders `secret 0` in the plan and the re-render alike. Every other render is unchanged (pinned). Controls: the plan and the re-render each fail when left on the platform's rule. |
| C53 | The CLI rotation path never saved the device, so a guest reload booted NVRAM, and nothing measured what NVRAM held. | 2026-09-27 | **Measured, fixed on the one device it was false for, and built.** Measured on the fleet (read-only): eight devices carried their running credential, and `s1` booted the credential the terminal EXPOSED (B13). `nmas-persist-native s1 --list Default` (the operator's) gave PERSISTED and ACCEPTED, and the re-run read **nine of nine CARRY**, the first time the property has held across the fleet by measurement rather than by accident on eight. Built: the persistence chain's FIRST stage is now `device_startup_config` (save on the device, read its startup config back), so the next CLI rotation cannot recreate the state. `nmas-startup-check` asks every device hourly, read-only, and job health names any device that would boot a credential NMAS does not hold. It asks the DEVICE, so it does not care which lab a device is in, or whether it is in one (C50's `kind: native` half). The timer is the operator's to install ([DEPLOY_LINUX.md](DEPLOY_LINUX.md)); until then its unit row reads `not_installed`. |
| C49 | A reservation Kea is told about could vanish at its next restart and look written until then: `kea-dhcp4.conf` is root-owned, so `config-write` cannot persist and `config-set` lives in memory only. | 2026-09-26 | **Built (P.6 D1) and demonstrated (M5).** Subnet 255 includes `/etc/kea/nmas/reservations-255.json`, a file the tool owns (`0755`/`0644`, because Kea's AppArmor profile holds a root `kea-dhcp4 -t` to the mode bits). M5 wrote `.50` into the fragment only and `.51` through `config-set` only: after a restart `.50` remained and `.51` was gone, the failure and its fix against one event in one run. The show before the reload read 0, so the instrument reads the daemon and not the file. [P6_ZTP_PROBE.md](P6_ZTP_PROBE.md) M5. |
| D2 | Whether a never-reached device should bind to a template was undecided, because binding took its platform's deploy path offline. | 2026-09-26 | **Resolved by scheme 3 (P.5).** A pending device is still bound (the bound set is computed from the manifest), but no device is part of the approval's fingerprint, so onboarding revokes nothing, and the approve route records a bound device with no capture as not validated instead of refusing. The tests that pinned the scheme-2 behaviour were flipped with the reason. |
| D11 | Template approval was all-or-nothing per platform, and its device-set half duplicated the per-device gate. | 2026-09-26 | **Built: scheme 3 (NSOT_PLAN P.5).** Approval is the template's closure hash and who approved it; per-device fidelity stays where it already gated, live, in `blocking_reasons` (measured before the change). Approving needs one validated device and records every device's result as evidence; the badge says what an approval covers and what it does not. |
| C46 | "No test touches a live network" was enforced for nothing a test STARTS, and one test's child asked the live NMAS for its map on every host run. | 2026-09-26 | **Built, three layers, on every machine.** (1) The test process refuses any non-loopback connect (an audit hook measured NONE before it was added, with a positive control). (2) `scripts/nmas-test` runs the suite in a loopback-only network namespace, and CI requires it. (3) **Every process a test starts, BY CONSTRUCTION:** a wrapper on `subprocess.Popen` (measured to be the only spawn path under test) rewrites every child's environment, an explicit one included: Python children load a `sitecustomize` refusing non-loopback connects and DNS names, `ssh`/`scp`/`sftp`/`rsync`/`curl`/`wget`/`nc`/`ncat`/`telnet` resolve to refusing shims (deferring only to a fake the test built under pytest's own temporary tree), and git may use local paths only. Every attempt is recorded and FAILS the test that made it, by name, because a child's own error is often swallowed by the code under test, which is how the original request went unseen. C46's exact case is a test. Six controls, each failing its target. **The host namespace was DECLINED (the operator, 2026-09-26), so it is not re-proposed without new facts:** a sudoers helper is narrow, but it is a root-owned path invoked by the TEST HARNESS on the machine that holds `key.key` and reaches every device, and the harness is exactly what did something nobody expected here; lifting the AppArmor restriction host-wide is worse. "NOT CONFINED, and it says so" is the honest state on the host, layer 3 covers it by construction, and the namespace runs in CI where it is free. |

Shells are keyed `<socket sid>|<ip>`, output goes to the owning connection's room only, a closed tab closes that connection's shells alone, and the record is one row per connection. The CURRENT case was measured the same way the run before (`already at d01e0b2`, true against `ls-remote`). **Recorded explanation (not proven): a race.** All three reports came while this session's commits were being pushed through the host relay, so "already current" was true when printed and false a minute later. **A future sighting is not a new bug by default**: check first whether a push landed after the deploy's fetch. The operator added operand printing to `~/bin/nmas-deploy` (2026-09-26), so any recurrence prints HEAD, origin/main and GitHub, and is self-evident rather than an argument. Three copies of 363,799 bytes. Restore test PASS (198 tables, 3,143 rows). The off-box copy was fetched with the read key and decrypted on the laptop. A broken destination is named by `nmas-jobs`. | C22 | rclone's exit code proved neither permission nor transfer, and the off-box push trusted it. | 2026-09-26 | **Fixed.** Measured by the operator: a refused read was retried ten times (`401`), then rclone printed `There was nothing to transfer` and exited 0, with the 401 visible only at `-vv`. The push path did not read, so it was unaffected on the day. But it confirmed its upload by exit code alone, and so would anything added later. Now every push is confirmed by LISTING `daily/` (a directory listing, which needs only `listFiles`; a single-file path would HEAD and need `readFiles`) and requiring the object at the artefact's exact size. An enabled prune is confirmed by listing too. The real `lsjson` shape was measured on the host before relying on it. Four controls. |
| B7 | `vmdata` became thin-provisioned and nothing watched its real allocation. | 2026-09-25 | **Built and live.** `job_health` reads `disks/zfs` (ALLOC/SIZE, what `zpool list` prints; readable by the auditor token, measured) and reports each pool: `pool_degrading` at 80 % (a convention, named as one), `pool_will_pause` when the headroom above ZFS's write reserve (1/32 of the pool, capped at 128 GiB) falls under 5 % of the pool, and `pool_unhealthy` for any health but ONLINE. An empty answer is `unknown`, never "no pools". Live: `vmdata 8.6% allocated (79.4 GiB of 928.0 GiB), frag 27%, ONLINE; 819.6 GiB before ZFS refuses writes`. Four negative controls, including substituting Proxmox's reservation percentage for ALLOC/SIZE. The thresholds come from OpenZFS and QEMU defaults, not from measuring a pool filling here. |

A copy of `data/` exists in B6's nightly image. The two were tied together by the restore test: the fingerprint of `key.key` inside the restored clone equals the escrowed `f42bddcea15442c2`, and the clone's app started on it. |

Both images were taken with the guest agent's fs-freeze. `job_health` watches it through a read-only token: per VM from the task logs, the storage (`inactive` / `will_not_fit` / `images_missing`) and the thin pool. The unmount check read `inactive`. **The test restore passed on the real image**: 9102 up with its links down, postgres recovered, HTTP 200, and the key fingerprint matched the escrow. | C21 | The heartbeat measurement AND the alert rules counted any line that merely named the applet. | 2026-09-25 | **Fixed.** Found because the check correctly reported s4 INSEPARABLE after the alert test. The long gap from the silence (2,925 s) WAS excluded. The cause was a SHORT one: 73 s, ending at `%HA_EM-4-FMPD_NO_EVENT: No event configured for applet NMAS-HEARTBEAT`, which the substring query `|= "NMAS-HEARTBEAT"` counted as a heartbeat. The alert rules use the same query, so a broken applet's own error read as a sign of life: the unsafe direction. Now anchored on the line's exact form, `%HA_EM-\d-LOG: NMAS-HEARTBEAT: NMAS-HEARTBEAT`. Over 6 h of the whole fleet those were the only two shapes naming the applet. **Also:** every gap spanning the device's own `%SYS-5-CONFIG_I` is excluded, because re-entering the applet restarts its timer and leaves a gap between one and two intervals: under the long-gap cut, and enough to make any maintenance window leave INSEPARABLE behind it. Re-measured live: all nine devices `measured`, s4 at window 1005 s. **The generated rules file was an untracked file in the live checkout** (the script's default output path), so committing it would have aborted the next pull. It is now gitignored, and `--check`'s help no longer calls it "committed". **Operator step:** regenerate and reinstall the rules; the installed ones still carry the substring query. |
| C20 | A settings-route test failed intermittently, echoing `kea_username` as `''`. | 2026-09-25 | **Two causes, both measured and fixed, neither the one first suspected.** **(1) The test failure: import-time binding.** `modules.integrations.base` binds `get_setting` by name. When `test_onboard_plan.py` ran before anything imported `app`, `base` was first imported inside that file's `lab` fixture, while `settings_schema.get_setting` was a lambda answering unlisted keys with the schema default. `base` kept the lambda. An import hook recorded the import, on the main thread, with the lambda bound. The order was deterministic: 3 of 3 failed with that file first, 2 of 2 passed with it second. The **streaks were my subset command**: `grep` in the tool shell is a parallel `ugrep` and returns files in varying order. `conftest.py` now imports `app` before any test runs, as the program does, and a subprocess test runs the failing order. **(2) Found while chasing it, and worse: concurrent settings writes CORRUPTED the file.** A probe recorded every write during a failing run and saw only the route's own two, both correct, so the redaction refresh and the background threads were ruled out by measurement. But `save_user_settings` used one temp name for every writer, and no read-modify-write held a lock. Two threads × 150 writes left `user_settings.json` unreadable in 2 of 2 runs, and the unreadable-file guard then refused every write. Fixed: a per-write `mkstemp`, and `config.settings_lock()` (an RLock, plus `flock` across processes) around all four read-modify-write sites. After the fix: 300 of 300 keys, threads and processes, 3 of 3 runs. An AST scan requires the lock in any function that both loads and saves. The general-settings POST was one of the four, and it **bypasses `write_settings()`**, so CLAUDE.md's *one write path* was not true. |
| B4 | NetBox's env files were world-readable (0664). | 2026-09-25 | **Closed on evidence.** The operator ran `chmod 600`; measured later that evening, all four `env/*.env` read `600` and `netbox-docker-netbox-1` was `Up 6 hours (healthy)`. NetBox also answered the heartbeat generator's queries after the change. The backup writes its copies `0600` regardless (pinned). |
| C18 | Commits from a CLI never pushed. | 2026-09-25 | **Fixed.** Post-commit hooks run on daemon threads, and a short-lived process (`nmas-retire`) exited under its own push: r5's retire commit `3592113` never reached `<repo>`, and nothing recorded a failure. Hook runners are now joined at exit, bounded at 90 s; an unfinished one is named on stderr as "the commit is local and may not have been pushed". Proven in a real subprocess, with a control. `3592113` pushes with the next commit from the app, or by hand. |
| C19 | Secret stores the checker could not see, and their modes. | 2026-09-25 | **Fixed in code.** `nmas-check-secret-storage` classifies EVERY file under `data/` (secret: must be 0600 / no-secret: scanned / config-bearing / plain), and an undeclared file fails the run, so a new store is noticed on its first run. Outside `data/` it checks a declared list (rclone.conf, the NetBox env files, the backup push key). Live: 773 files classified, 0 unclassified, and **5 loose secret stores found**: `credential_profiles.json`, `devices.csv` plus two old copies, and `secret.key` (Flask's session key), all 0664, beneath a run that had printed "every file holding a secret is owner-only". The credential store's and devices.csv's writers and `secret.key`'s creation now go through `open_secure`, so the next write heals them. The two old CSV copies and `secret.key` are never rewritten, so they need `chmod 600` by hand. Actual exposure was limited: `data/` is 0700. |
| C16 | The per-platform heartbeat window was the wrong shape. | 2026-09-25 | **Built: per-device windows** (`scripts/nmas-heartbeat-rules`). Each device's window is the midpoint of its own separable band, measured from its Loki arrivals, with misses in the history excluded. A new device gets a **provisional** window, labelled as such in the rule itself and derived from the fleet's slowest measured rate; a device with an empty band is named `inseparable`. `--check` re-measures hourly on its own timer, declared in `job_health`, and names `STALE RATE`, `PROVISIONAL READY`, `UNMEASURABLE`, `MISSING` and `EXTRA`. **Live: nine measured windows**, r1–r4 and r6 750 s, s1 797, s2 799, s3 1286, s4 996; r5 named not expected; `--check` current for all nine. Controls shown failing. |
| C15 | A write whose commit failed was stranded, and the run that stranded it reported success. | 2026-09-25 | **Fixed in `scripts/oxidized-to-config.sh`.** Every run reconciles every managed file BEFORE the early exit and before any copy. A dirty file is committed ("recorded late") only when it is byte-identical to `render_device`'s output for the Oxidized version its own header names; anything else is REFUSED for that device, neither committed nor overwritten, named, and the run exits 3 (including on the "nothing to copy" path). The per-lab commit now adds and commits only the job's own files by path. The executed tests found their own fixture could not exhibit the version case (its second poll changed only a stripped comment); fixed, and the control then fired. r6's stranded file was committed by hand by the operator. |
| C11 | Deleting a device left it half-managed in five places; there was no retire. | 2026-09-25 | **Built: `scripts/nmas-retire` / `modules/nsot/retire.py`.** Clear the override, declare the startup file deliberately unmapped, then one commit that removes intent and golden and releases the identity (history kept), then the CSV row LAST, and only against a break-glass record holding the device's CURRENT credential (a refusal, not a step). It states every time what it will NOT do: NetBox kept, Oxidized polling, startup frozen, device config unchanged, backups kept, the pooled session. It is resumable, and a failed commit puts the tree back. **ACCEPTANCE PASSED 2026-09-25 on the real device (operator):** commit `3592113`; zero r5 in host_vars, golden, devices.csv and the manifest; the approval withdrawn naming its cause ("device 'r5' is no longer bound"); `cisco_iosxe/base.j2` binds r1, r2, r3, r4, r6; credential overrides: 1 (r6's), 0 unclaimed. Declared-unmapped on its first real use: `produced : 9`, `declared : 1 — r5: ISP device outside our administrative boundary… (<user>, 2026-09-25T20:33:27Z)`. Found beside it and fixed: `nmas-clab-targets --device` unpacked 3 columns from 6-column rows and crashed on the first. |
| D3 | The template editor's confirmations did not say what happened. | 2026-09-25 | **Fixed.** The save message is built from the route's `approval_revoked` and `revoked`; the approval cell draws the reason and then what re-approval needs; `_common.j2` is listed as a shared file (Edit only, with what editing it revokes). Three pure renderers are executed in duktape (`test_template_library_renders.py`), with controls shown failing. The operator's step 3 had already shown the underlying state was right; only the panel was not. |
| C4 | `health()` is per-process. | 2026-09-25 | **Decided and built.** The counters stay in-process; `health()` now carries `pid` and `counting_since` (when counting began), and the CLI prints both beside the counts. Durable counts come from `logs/device_manager.log`, which the CLI reads with a denominator. |
| C3 | *"The recorder missed a confirmed write."* | 2026-09-25 | **Measured false.** The record holds the entry (`r1 comments … TEST → …`, `06:34:40Z`), the file's mtime is 0.18 s after NetBox's `last_updated`, the reader lists it, and the app log holds 0 recorder ERROR lines in 549,266. The positive control **passed**; it was read as failing. The misreading's mechanism is undetermined — the entry is the newest of 44, printed last, so any view of the output's head would omit it, but that is a candidate, not a measurement. [PHASE2_DHCP.md](PHASE2_DHCP.md) §23. |
